Liber Genesis
by Anselm of Laon and his school
Source text: Public Domain. English translation/apparatus/encoding: CC BY-NC 4.0, Wroot Press.

----------------------------------------

Called in Hebrew בראשית , BERESIT, that is In the beginning; in Greek ΓΕΝΕΣΙΣ, that is Generation.

## CHAPTER ONE.

[0067B]

VERS. 1.-- « In the beginning he created, » etc. He does not say: In the beginning God says, Let heaven and earth be made. But he said: « Let there be light, and light was made, » because under the name of heaven and earth there was to be comprehended universally whatsoever God made, and thereafter it was to be set forth part by part how he made it. Whence there follows: « God said, Let it be made, » that is, by his Word he made it. Or because, when formless matter, spiritual or corporeal, was first coming to be, it was not to be said: « God said, Let it be made; » for it was unfitting that « let it be made » should be said by God, since imperfection does not imitate the form of the Word, save when, by the conversion of its own kind to the Creator, the creature is made perfect; so that when it is said: « God said, Let it be made, » we may understand that he calls back to himself the imperfection of the creature. [0067C] When therefore it is said: « In the beginning God made heaven and earth, » [var: Vulg. Gen. 1:1 *creavit*, "created"] there is a commemoration of the Son, because he is the beginning; but when it is said: « God said, Let it be made, » it is commemorated that he is the Word. By "beginning" he notes the origin of the creature that exists from him; by "the Word," the perfecting of the creature called back from him to himself, that it may be formed by imitating the unchangeable form of the Word. For the Word of God has not a formless life, to whom to be is the same as to live; nay rather, to live the same as to live blessedly. But the spiritual creature [0068B] has a formless life, to whom to be is not the same as to live; nor to live the same as to live blessedly: because, turned away from the wisdom of God, it lives foolishly and wretchedly.

« In the beginning, » etc. [n: (BEDA, Hexaem. lib. I, tom. II, col. 13 ª.)] Scripture, in intimating the creation of the world, shows by its first word the eternity and the omnipotence of God: for him whom it declares to have created the world in the beginning of times, it signifies to have existed eternally before times. And him whom it relates to have created heaven and earth at the outset of the founding, it declares to be omnipotent by so great a swiftness of working, for whom to have willed is to do. Well therefore is it said: « In the beginning he created, » etc. Both, that is, at once, although both are not uttered at once by a man. Whence the prophet shows the earth to have been made in the beginning, saying, [0068C] psal. CI: « In the beginning thou, O Lord, didst found the earth, » etc.

« In the beginning, » etc. Heaven, not the visible firmament, but the empyrean, that is, the fiery or intellectual heaven, which is so called not from burning but from splendour, and which was straightway filled with angels. Whence in Job XXXVIII: « Where wast thou when the morning stars praised me, » etc. And note that three elements are here commemorated. By the name of heaven we understand the air. By the name of earth, the earth itself and the fire that lies hid in it. Of the fourth, that is, of water, mention is made in what follows.

[0069A] [n: (ALCUIN. in Gen. tom. I.)] « In the beginning. » In the Son, by whose being made man it lay open who were the heavenly and who the earthly. Heaven, the spiritual creature, from its beginning perfect and blessed. Earth, corporeal matter, as yet imperfect. Heaven, the formless matter of spiritual life, such as it can exist in itself, not turned to the Creator in whom it is formed. Earth, the corporeal, without any quality that appears in formed matter.

[n: (BEDA in Gen., tom. II, col. 191.)] Scripture says: « Who madest the world of formless matter [n: (Sap. XI)]. » [var: Vulg. Sap. 11:18 *quae creavit orbem terrarum ex materia invisa*, "which created the world out of matter unseen"] Matter was made out of nothing. The species of the world, out of formless matter. Accordingly God made two things before all time, the angelic creature and formless matter. For although Solomon says: « Who lives for [0069B] « ever created all things together [n: (Eccli. XVIII)], yet according to origin formless matter goes before formed species. And so God made the world, and the angels, and the soul out of nothing: man and the remaining creatures out of something.

[n: (Ibid., col. 192.)] There are seven heavens, whose names are these, air, ether, olympus, the space or fiery region, the firmament, the heaven of the angels, and that of the Trinity. Jerome, however, says that the heaven of the Trinity is the first, that of the angels the second, the firmament the third.

*Mystically.* « In the beginning God created heaven. » Those, namely, who have borne the image of the heavenly one. « And the earth, » that is, those who afterwards, by growing proud, bearing the earth, that is, the image of the earthly man, made themselves deformed. « But the earth was void, » because [0069C] it had laid aside its good form. « And empty, » of the fruit of good work. « And there were darknesses, » the privation, namely, of the true light; « upon the face of the deep, » the hearts, namely, of the proud.

VERS. 2.-- « But the earth was void, » etc. [n: (BEDA, Hexaem. tom. II.)] He shows what heaven, or what kind of heaven, was made in the beginning together with the earth. For he subjoins that of the earth which he did not wish to be understood of heaven. For this higher heaven, which is set apart from the world's revolving, as soon as it was created was filled with holy angels: whom the Lord testifies to have been founded in the beginning together with heaven and earth, saying, Job XXXVIII: « Where wast thou when the morning stars praised me, and all the sons of God rejoiced? » He calls the same angels both morning stars and sons of God. For of the heaven in [0069D] which the luminaries are set, how or when it was made, he tells afterwards.

[n: (STRAB.)] « Void and empty. » Useless, namely, and unfruitful, and unordered. For all the elements were mingled and confused, and this whole space of air was full of waters; not such as they now are, but they were like thin clouds, such as those above the heavens still are.

[n: (BEDA ubi supra.)] « There were darknesses, » etc. Those are not to be heard who say by way of reproach that God created darkness before light: because he made no darkness in the water or in the air; but by a distinct order of providence he first created the waters together with the heaven, and the earth, and adorned these with the grace of light when he willed. And it is to be noted that with the heaven two elements of the world were created, [0070A] into which two others were inserted, water, namely, and earth, in which are fire and air. And the waters covered the whole surface of the earth with so great a depth that they reached even to those places where until now they partly rest above the firmament. But the earth itself and the water are called formless matter, because all the things which we see took their beginning either from these or out of nothing: and before they came into the light, there was not whence they might have form.

« And the Spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters. » [var: Vulg. Gen. 1:2 *Spiritus Dei*, "the Spirit of God"] [n: (AUG., lib. I de Gen. ad lit., c. 5, tom. III.)] Either he called the whole corporeal matter water, that he might intimate whence were made and formed all the things which we see formed on the earth out of a moist nature through divers species; or the spiritual life, as it were floating, before the [0070B] form of its conversion. The Spirit of God was borne above, because that which he had begun to be formed and perfected lay beneath the good will of the Creator. It is also said, « The Spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters. » Lest, by doing works, God should be thought to love through the necessity of want rather than through the abundance of beneficence. For fittingly is something first intimated to have been begun, over which he might be borne: not in place, but by a power surpassing all things.

« And the Spirit of the Lord was borne, » etc. [n: (HIERON. in Gen. t. III, col. 939.)] In the Hebrew it has *merahephet,* that is, it brooded, or cherished, after the manner of a bird warming eggs with the heat of a living thing. We understand therefore that this is not said of the spirit of the world, as many suppose; but of the [0070C] Holy Spirit, who is himself the quickener of all things: and if the quickener, also the founder; if the founder, also God. Psal. CIII: « Send forth, he says, thy Spirit, and they shall be created. » [var: Vulg. Ps. 103:30 *Emittes*, "Thou shalt send forth"]

« And the Spirit, » etc. In whom, subsisting, we might rest, quickened by his breath and washed by the wave of baptism. « And the Spirit of the Lord was borne over the waters, » etc. As God, namely, and Creator, he presided over the floating and confused matter, that he might distinguish it when he willed. The whole Trinity therefore is understood to have worked here, God, that is, the Father; the beginning, the Son; the Spirit of God, the Holy Spirit.

« And the Spirit, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen.? )] The hearts, namely, that were tossing to and fro, which had lost the quiet of the mind, [0070D] because the Spirit did not rest upon them, but was borne above them: as one able to call back their nature from confusion into something better. Then throughout the whole text of this Scripture we see certain six laborious ages, though distinguished as it were by neighbouring bounds, so that in the seventh rest may be hoped for; and we see that they have a likeness to the six days in which those things were made which Scripture records God to have made. For the first beginning of the human race, in which it began to enjoy this light, is compared to the first day, on which God made light. This age is as it were the infancy of the whole world, which we think of, by the proportion of its greatness, as one man. For man, when he is first born into the light, passes his infancy, the first age, which extends from Adam to Noah in ten generations: the flood is as it were the evening of this [0071A] day, because our infancy is blotted out by the flood of forgetfulness.

VERS. 3.-- « And God said: Let there be light. » [n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. I, c. 17, tom. III.)] The light made on the first day is understood as spiritual or corporeal, etc., *as far as* the beginning of a morning to come. [n: (BEDA, Hexaem. )] But if corporeal light was made on the first day, fittingly is the adorning of the world begun from light, by which the rest, which were to be created, might be seen. But if it be asked: where was it made, since the deep covered the whole height of the earth? it is plain that it was made in those parts which the daily light of the sun now illumines. Nor is it a wonder that light can shine in waters, since they are even oftentimes lit up by the working of sailors, who, plunged in the deep, having let oil out of the mouth, light up the waters for themselves: which waters in the beginning were far rarer [0071B] than they now are, because they were not yet gathered into one place.

« And God said: Let there be light. » [n: (AUG. ibid., c. 2.)] Not temporally: for if temporally, then changeably also; if changeably, then also through a subject creature, and light is not the first creature. But perhaps, etc., *as far as* that it may be and that it may abide.

« Let there be light. » [n: (AUG. ibid., c. 4, 5, 9.)] On that condition, namely, by which all things subsist timelessly in the wisdom of God before they subsist in themselves. « And light was made, » that is, the angelic and heavenly substance, in itself temporally; as it was in wisdom, so far as regards his unchangeableness, eternally. Or there is noted the formlessness, namely, and the imperfection of this creature before it was formed in the love of its Founder: for it is formed when [0071C] it is turned to the unchangeable light of the Word.

« Let there be light. » [n: (AUG. ibid., c. 10, 11, 16.)] If light was made corporeally, etc., *as far as* for there were not yet animals to which this vicissitude might be exhibited.

VERS. 4.-- « And God saw the light, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] God made light and darkness, whence Dan. III. « Bless the Lord, O light and darkness, » when every creature is invited to bless.

[n: (ISID., in Gen., c. 2.)] Each one has in his works and in an upright life six distinct days, after which he may hope for rest: on the first day, the light of faith, since he first believes in invisible things, for which faith the Lord deigned to appear.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

« And the evening was made. » [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. I, c. 4; BEDA, in Hexaem.)] The light going down [0071D] little by little, and after the space of the divine length passing under to the lower parts, the evening was made, as it is now wont to come about by the accustomed circuit of the sun. And the morning was made when the same light returned over the earth and began another day, and one day of twenty-four hours was completed. But it is to be noted that the night in that three days' space was wholly dark, which after the stars were created shone with some light: it was fitting also that the days should begin from light and stretch toward the morning of the following day, so that the works of God might be signified to have been begun from light and completed unto light.

VERS. 5.--. . . . « And the evening was made, » etc. [n: (GREG., l. VIII Moral., c. 6, tom. I.)] In no wise in this life is sin so forsaken by the exercise of justice [0072A] that one may abide in it unshaken: because, if uprightness drives out guilt, guilt sits at the doors of our thought and knocks, that it may be opened to it. Whence Moses says: « Light was made; » and a little after: « The evening was made. » For the Creator, foreknowing man's guilt, expressed then in time what is now busied in the mind. For the shadow of temptation follows the light of uprightness. But because the light of the elect is not put out by temptation, not night but evening is recorded to have been made, because temptation hides the light of justice, it does not destroy it.

[n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l. IV, c. 22, 23, tom. III, col. 311, 312.)] It is to be noted that the other creatures, etc., *as far as* that the one, namely, may belong to the day, this to the evening.

[0072B]VERS. 6.--. . . « Let there be a firmament, » etc. [n: (GREG., l. XXVII Moral., c. 24.)] The angelic powers which stood fast in the love of God received this in retribution, that they should abide in the contemplation of their Founder in everlasting happiness, and should subsist eternally in that wherein they were founded. Whence in Moses the heaven is said to have been made, and the same is afterwards called the firmament, because the angelic nature was first founded subtle in the higher places, and afterwards, that it might never be able to fall, was more wonderfully confirmed.

« Let there be a firmament, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 2, col. 264.)] It is asked whether that heaven which exceeds the spaces of the air is now made, etc., *as far as* because its interval divides between certain vapours of the waters and the waters which are on the earth.

[0072C] « Let there be a firmament, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid., c. 4.)] It is to be noted that the firmament is not so called on account of standing still, etc., *as far as* but in whatever manner they are there, that they are there we do not doubt.

« Let there be a firmament in the midst. » [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem., t. II.)] Of the heaven, in which the stars are fixed, etc., *as far as* But of what sort they are, and for what they are useful, he knows who founded them.

VERS. 7.-- « And God made the firmament, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 8, col. 269.)] It is asked why, when he had said: « Let there be a firmament, and it was so done, » he afterwards adds: « And God made the firmament, » etc., *as far as* saying: « And God said, Let it be made, » etc.

[n: (Ibid., c. 1, 3, 5.)] Above the air is pure fire, etc., *as far as* The nearness of the supercelestial waters therefore makes it [0072D] cold.

[n: (Ibid.)] But it is to be noted that on the first day, when light was made, it was said only: « Let there be light, » and this in the word, and « light was made, » in the work, and straightway the created light clave to the uncreated light, as nearer to it and more capacious than every creature. On the second day there is a certain delay, and a multiplying of words, « let it be made, » and « God made, » and « it was so done » : that we may understand these natures to be prone to fail, and less apt to obey.

[n: (HIERON., l. I contra Jovin., t. II, col. 211.)] It is also to be noted that the works of this second day are not said, according to the Hebrews, to be good, although they are good like the rest: which comes about on account of the binary number, the chief of alternation, [0073A] which first departs from unity: and it is set as a figure of bigamy and of many reprehensible things; whence the things which enter into the ark two by two are unclean, an odd number clean. Or they are not said to be good for this reason, that in this work the separation of the water is not made perfect, which is made in the next, where he says: « Let the waters be gathered together, » as will be said there.

[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 9.)] It is wont to be asked of what shape the heaven is: but the Holy Spirit, although our authors knew, did not will to teach through them save what profits salvation. It is also asked: does the heaven stand still, or is it moved? If it is moved, they say, how is it a firmament? If it stands still, how do the stars fixed in it go round? But the firmament can be so called not on account of standing still, but on account of firmness, or the untransgressible boundary [0073B] of the waters. But if it stands still, nothing hinders the stars from being moved and going round. On the second day there was made as it were the firmament of discipline, which distinguishes between carnal and spiritual things, as between the lower and the higher waters.

[n: (ISID., in Gen. c. 2, tom. V, col. 213.)] On the second day he set the firmament, that is, the holy Scriptures in the Church. Of which it is said, Isa. XXXIV: « The heaven shall be folded together like a book. » By this firmament he divides the higher waters, that is, the people of the angels, for whom there is no need that they should hear the word of God in reading. But he set that firmament above the infirmity of the lower peoples, that looking up at it they may know how it distinguishes between carnal and spiritual things, as it were between the higher and the lower waters.

[0073C] « And he divided the waters which were, » etc. [n: (AMBR., l. II Hexaem., c. 3, tom. I, col. 148.)] When thou hearest these things, why dost thou marvel if above the firmament a wave could be hung? etc., *as far as* Jordan also, its stream turned back, returns into its own spring.

VERS. 8.--. . . . « And the evening was made, » etc. [n: (ISID., ubi supra.)] The morning of the second day begins from Noah, etc., *as far as* because neither is boyhood apt for generation.

VERS. 9.-- « And God said, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 11, tom. III.)] On this day it seems to have been done, etc., *as far as* Whence the water is gathered together, the earth appears; for water is slippery and flowing, earth stable and fixed.

« Let the waters be gathered together? » [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem. )] The waters which had filled all things between heaven and earth are gathered [0073D] into one place, that the light, which in the two days past had lit up the waters with a clear light, might shine brighter in the pure air; and that the earth might appear, which lay hidden; and that what was slimy with waters might become dry and apt for shoots. If it be asked where the waters were gathered together which had covered all parts of the earth up to the heaven, it could come about that the earth, subsiding, afforded hollow parts by which it might receive the floating waters: it can also be believed that the first waters were rarer, which covered the lands like a cloud, but that by the gathering they were thickened.

VERS. 10.-- « And God called the dry land Earth. » [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] Before, for the distinguishing of the waters, he called the whole more solid part of the world earth, saying: « In the beginning God created heaven and earth. » After [0074A] the world now begins to be formed, and the face of the earth appears, the waters withdrawing, for the distinguishing of the part which was still pressed down by the waters, the portion which it was permitted to be dry received the name of earth, in that it is trodden by the feet. « And the gatherings together of the waters he called Seas. » For so among the Hebrews all gatherings of waters are called, whether salt or sweet. And because, by the continuity of all the waters which are on the earth, he said that they were gathered into one place, he now calls them in the plural the gatherings of waters, on account of their manifold bays. Although it is agreed that there are many seas, yet he says the waters were gathered into one place, because all are joined to the great sea. If any lakes seem to be shut up in themselves, yet by hidden passages they are rolled back into the sea: the diggers of wells [0074B] also prove this, because all the ground is filled with waters through invisible veins, whose beginning is from the sea.

VERS. 11.-- « And he said: Let the earth bring forth, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 12.)] The governance of the Ordainer is to be marked: although the creature of herbs and of trees is distinct from the kind of the lands and of the waters, so that they cannot be numbered among the elements: yet because, fixed by their roots, they are bound to the earth, he willed them to belong to the same day.

[n: (BEDA, ubi supra.)] It is plain from these words that the world was perfected and adorned in the springtime, in which green herbs are wont to appear, and trees laden with fruits. And at the same time it is to be noted that the first shoots of herbs or kinds of trees came forth not from seed, but from the earth: because at one command of the Founder there appeared [0074C] the dry land trimmed with herbs and clothed with groves, which of themselves brought forth the fruits and seeds of their own kind. For it behoved that the form of things should come forth perfect at God's first command: as man, for whose sake are all things, was fashioned in a youthful age.

[n: (AUG., ibid. l. III, c. 18.)] It is also asked concerning thorns, and thistles, and certain unfruitful trees, why or when they were created? since God said: « Let the earth bring forth the green herb, » etc. But by the name of fruit a certain usefulness is signified: and there are many usefulnesses, manifest or hidden, of all the things which the earth nourishes by their roots. We can, however, answer without qualification that after sin the earth brought forth thorns and thistles for man's labour: not that they were born elsewhere before, and afterwards in the fields which man tilled: but before and afterwards in the same [0074D] places, yet before not for man, but afterwards. Whence « it shall bring forth to thee, » that is, that there should begin to be born for thee unto labour the things which before were born only for the food of other animals.

« Making seed. » [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] All things were created perfect, and man was perfect in age, and the trees with leaves and fruit: whence this is called a « tree bearing fruit. »

VERS. 12.-- « And the earth brought forth the herb, » etc. [n: (GREG., l. XIX Moral., c. 12; l. VI, c. 16, tom. I.)] What history testifies was truly so done, that it might signify something else to be done. For the earth is the Church, which refreshes us with the food of the word, and guards us with the shade of its patronage: by speaking it feeds, by helping it protects; [0075A] so that it may bring forth not only the herb of refreshment, but also the tree of protection.-- « And the tree. » The tree brought forth seed according to its own kind, when our mind gathers from the consideration of itself what it should do toward another, and bears the shoot of upright work. Hence a certain wise man says: « What thou wouldest not have done to thee, do not thou to another [n: (Tob. IV)]. » And elsewhere: « Whatsoever you would that men should do to you, do you also the same to them [n: (Matth. VII)]. » As if to say, look upon one another as your own species, and know from your own selves what it behoves you to show to others.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

Seed. » [n: (STRAB., RAB.)] "Sementis" is of crops and of trees; "semen," of men and of animals. "Seminium" or "sementum," the beginning of anything whatever. On the third day, on which a man prepares his mind for the sowing of the [0075B] fruits of good works: the taint being separated, and the waves of carnal temptations, he has as it were dry land, temptations being set apart, so that he can say: « I serve the law of God with the mind, » etc. [n: (Rom. VII)].

VERS. 13.-- « And the evening and the morning were made. » [n: (ISID. in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.)] The morning of the third day begins from Abraham, etc., *as far as* The evening in the sins of the people, by which they were transgressing the divine commandments, until the wickedness of Saul.

[n: (ISID., ibid.)] On the third day he gathered together the lower and salt waters, that is, the unbelievers, shaking themselves with the waves of concupiscences and of temptations: and he set apart from them the dry land, that is, the faithful thirsting for the fountain of faith. But the unbelievers he restrained, lest with the waves of their iniquity they should trouble the dry land, that is, the soul thirsting for God, [0075C] but that it might be free for it to bring forth the fruits of good works, and to love its neighbour, so that it may have in itself seed according to its own likeness; that is, that out of its own weakness it may have compassion on another in need, and may produce a strong fruit-bearing tree, that is, a beneficent one, by rescuing the oppressed and affording him the shade of its protection.

VERS. 14.-- « But God said: Let there be luminaries, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. II, c. 13, 14.)] Because to the constituting of the visible world two days are assigned, namely to the highest and the lowest part of the world: to the visible and movable parts of the world, which are created within the world, the three remaining ones were allotted, etc., *as far as* unto the inchoation, or principate, of the night, which comes about when she is full.

And let them divide the day. » [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem.)] By that [0075D] division, namely, that the sun should light the day, the moon the night. For this was added, when the stars were made, to the increase of the first light, that the night also might come forth luminous, irradiated by the splendour of the moon or of the stars, which hitherto had known darkness only. And although the night seems dark because the air which is nearest to the lands is darkened, yet the higher spaces of the air are always bright with the brightness of the stars.

[n: (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 18.)] It is wont to be asked whether these luminaries are bodies only, or have ruling spirits, and are breathed upon by them vitally, as flesh is animated through the souls of animals. But concerning so obscure a matter nothing is rashly to be believed.

« And let them be for signs, » etc. [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] Because before [0076A] the stars were made, the order of the times was marked by no tokens, neither the midday hour nor any other. The luminaries therefore are « for signs, » etc.; not that from the founding of these the times, or the days, or the years began; but because by their risings, or their passings, the order of times, of days and of years is marked.

VERS. 15.-- « That they may shine, » etc. [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] The luminaries always shine in the firmament, but at fitting times they illumine the earth. For sometimes the cloudy air stands in the way: the sun also, having risen, hinders the moon and the stars with its greater light from illumining the earth. Whence it is so called, because, the moon and the other stars being dulled, it alone shines upon the lands by day.

VERS. 16.-- « God made two luminaries. » [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] We can take them to be great luminaries, not so much [0076B] by comparison with others as in themselves; as the heaven is called great, and the sea great. For the sun is called great, because it fills the globe of the earth with its heat: as does the moon with its light, which, in whatever part of the heaven they may be, illumine the whole earth, and are seen by all. A great argument of their greatness is that to all men the same orb of them appears: for if it were smaller to those placed afar and greater to those near, it would be an indication of littleness.

« The greater luminary, » etc. [n: (BEDA, ibid.)] The sun, namely, not only in the form of its body, but in the greatness of its light, because it lights up the lesser luminary and the stars. It is greater also in heat, because it warms the world, whereas in the days past before its creation it had had no heat. But that the moon is discerned to be of equal and of one greatness [0076C] with the sun, they say comes of this, that the sun goes far more distant from the lands and higher than the moon, and therefore the sun's greatness cannot be discerned by us. For all things set farther off appear smaller.

« And the lesser luminary, » etc. And although it sometimes happens that the moon and the greater stars are seen by day, yet it is agreed that they bring the solace of their light not to the day, but to the night.

[n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt. lib. II, c. 15, tom. III.)] For then, at the beginning of the night, the moon is seen, when she is full, at other times not: and by day she begins to be seen before her fulness, and in the progress of the night.

VERS. 17, 18-- . . . . « That they might shine, » etc. These things can be understood of the great luminaries and of the stars; so that what is said, « that they might rule the day, » should belong specially to the sun, [0076D] what is subjoined, « and the night, » to the moon and the stars; but what is added, « that they might divide the light and the darkness, » should suit all alike; which, wheresoever they go, carry light about them; whence, when they are absent, they leave all things dark. If it be asked what the daylight could have been like before the creation of the stars, we answer, such as we see when the rising of the sun is at hand, but it does not yet appear: when, namely, the day shines with the dulled rays of the stars, but the risen sun by no means blazes forth.

[n: (ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.)] On the fourth day the luminaries glittered in the firmament of heaven, etc., *as far as* because after good works there comes the illumination by which the species of the supernal truth is seen.

[0077A] [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I, c. 24, tom. III, col. 194.)] On the fourth day, on which in that firmament of discipline the mind works and distinguishes spiritual understandings, it sees what is the unchangeable truth, which shines like the sun in the soul; and how the soul is made partaker of it, and bestows order and beauty upon the body, as the moon lighting the night. And how all the stars, that is, spiritual understandings, shine in the obscurity of this life as it were in the night.

VERS. 19.-- « And the evening and the morning were made, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. II, col. 13.)] In this evening the people of God in Egypt offered the lamb, etc., *as far as* he might grant hope and faith.

« And the evening was made, » etc. [n: (ISID., ubi supra.)] [0077B] The morning of the fourth day, the kingdom of David. This age is like to youth, which reigns among all the ages, and is the firm firmament of all the ages: whence it is compared to the fourth day, on which the stars were made in the firmament of heaven. The splendour of the sun indeed figures the excellence of the kingdom, the splendour of the moon shows the people obeying the king, as it were the Synagogue. But the stars are its princes, and all things founded on the stability of the kingdom, as it were in the firmament.

VERS. 20.-- « God also said, » etc. [n: (BEDA, ubi supra.)] After the face of heaven had been adorned on the fourth day with luminaries, the lower parts of the world, namely water and air, are adorned on the fifth day with their own living things.

« God also said: Let the waters bring forth the creeping thing, » etc. *(AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., ex c. 1, 2, 3, 4, [0077C] 5 et 6, tom. III.)* It behoved, in the creating of the inhabitants of the lower part, etc., *as far as* Which is not to be understood save of such as have been turned into the nature of the waters by the quality of a thicker air.

« Of a living soul. » [n: (AUG., ibid., c. 8.)] That is, the things which are in living souls, as it is said: "The ignoble things of men," that is, those who among men are ignoble. Which some think was said on account of their slowness of sense, or because fishes are of no memory; but it is most certain that they have memory.

[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 3, 4.)] Concerning the conversion of the elements there is a question. Some say that all things can be turned into all things, etc., *as far as* for that we feel the waters and the winds by touch; hence it is that the solid earth is mingled with all the elements, but in these thicker ones it is felt [0077D] more.

« Under the firmament » , etc. Even though there lies between an immense space, yet the birds which fly above the earth fly under the starry heaven: just as men who are on the earth are said to be under heaven. Another translation: « Beside the firmament of heaven, » because the spaces of this air which bears up the birds are neighbours to the ether.

[n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l, III, c. 9, 10, tom. III, col. 284.)] It is to be noted that certain philosophers have so distributed the animals to each element, etc., *as far as* yet they have been permitted to hold only the misty places of the air, which is to them as it were a prison until the time of judgment.

VERS. 21.-- « And God created the great whales, » etc. [0078A] [n: (THEOD. q. 18 in Gen.)] Boys need now bugbears, now straps also and rods: by the former we frighten them, by the latter we instruct them: Since therefore God perceived that we too are prone to sloth, he founded the beasts, as it were certain straps and bugbears, that by frightening us with them he might draw us to himself, and that we might implore his help. But as those who are perfect despise bugbears of this sort, and straps; so those who have been brought up in virtue do not dread the onsets of beasts. Indeed the beasts stood by Adam before he had sinned, professing their obedience. And again the lion, the leopard, and all the fiercest of the beasts followed Noah himself as he entered the ark, after the manner of sheep. And the lions were present with Daniel, who, though otherwise famished, yet did not dare to approach him, because [0078B] they beheld in him the characters that bore before them the divine image. Likewise the viper, which had fastened its teeth in the hand of the Apostle, when it had found nothing in him tender or soft to sin, straightway leapt back and cast itself into the fire, exacting after a fashion punishment of itself, because it had attacked a body in no wise suited to it. But we for this cause dread the beasts, because we neglect virtue. Moreover the beasts are not useless to us for other things either: for out of them physicians compound not a few medicines for driving away diseases: let no one therefore, considering the beasts, complain of the Creator, but let him search out their usefulness.

« And every soul, » etc. In distinction from man, who was to be made to the image of God, and, if he kept the commandments, would remain in perpetual unchangeableness. [0078C] But the other animals were so made that some should yield to others for nourishment, or should fail through age.

VERS. 22.-- « And he blessed them, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. de Gen. imp., c. 15, tom. III.)] He willed the blessing to avail unto fruitfulness, which appears in the succession of offspring, so that by that blessing by which they were created weak and mortal, they might guard their own kind by being born.



## CHAPTER ONE. (cont.)

[n: (CHRYSOST., hom. 7 in Gen., tom. IV, col. 66.)] The blessing itself, therefore, is to be increased and multiplied in a great number and multitude. For because living creatures were animate, and willed to be perpetually, therefore he subjoined: « Increase. » For that word preserves them even unto the present day. « Increase and multiply, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem.)] It belongs to both [0078D] kinds of living things made out of the waters, that is, to fishes and to birds. For as all fishes cannot live save in the waters, so very many birds; which, if they sometimes rest on the lands and make their young there, yet live not from the land but from the sea, and use the sea more willingly than the land.

*Mystically. [n: (ISID. et AUG., ubi supra.)]* On the fifth day there were made in the waters the creeping things of living souls, that is, men renewed unto life through the sacrament of baptism. There were made flying things, that is, souls flying to the things above. On the fifth day the mind that has advanced begins to work in the actions of a turbulent world, as it were in the waters of the sea, for the profit of brotherly fellowship, and out of the corporeal acknowledgments which belong to the sea, that is, [0079A] to this life, to bring forth the creeping things of living souls, that is, works which profit living souls; and great whales, that is, the strongest acknowledgments, by which the waves of the world are broken and despised; and the flying things of heaven, that is, voices preaching heavenly things.

VERS. 23.-- « And the evening and the morning were made, etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 23, tom. III.)] Mystically, the morning is the passing over into Babylon, when by that captivity the people was lightly settled in a foreign leisure, etc., *as far as* because they were so blinded that they could not even know Christ.

VERS. 24.-- « God also said: Let the « earth bring forth, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III, col. 13.)] After the heaven was filled with stars and the air with flying things, which on account of its nearness is called heaven, etc., [0079B] *as far as* under the name of four-footed beasts all things are comprehended except the beasts and the creeping things, whether tamed, or wild and of the fields.

« Let the earth bring forth, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. III in Gen., c. 11, tom. III.)] It followed that he should now adorn the earth with its own animals. By saying three times, according to its own kind, etc., *as far as* And under the name of cattle and of beasts every irrational animal is wont to be comprehended. « In its own kind. » [n: (AUG., ibid. c. 12,)] These things are perhaps said according to kind, because they so arose that others are born of them, and preserve by succession the form of their origin. But of man this was not said, because one was being made, of whom the woman also was made. For there were not made many kinds of men, as of herbs and of trees; so that it should be said, according to its own kind, as [0079C] if it were said kind by kind, that among things alike, and belonging to one origin of seed, they might be distinguished from the rest.

[n: (AUG., ibid. c. 14.)] Concerning certain minute animals there is a question, etc., *as far as* except that they were fore-sown in them potentially and materially.

[n: (AUG., ibid. c. 15, 16.)] Concerning venomous and destructive creatures it is asked, etc., *as far as* all which, rightly considered, are praised.

[n: (AUG., ibid., c. 17.)] This also moves us, etc., *as far as* but let them not doubt to prepare the numbers of a godly fortitude for all things.

[n: (THEOD., quaest. 18 in Gen.)] Let us not despise the beasts, as being altogether unharmed by them, as though they were of no strength. Sometimes God permits two or [0079D] three out of many thousands either to be stung by scorpions or bitten by serpents; that we, fearing lest we should suffer anything like, may call upon God the Creator for help.

[n: (AUG. in Gen., l. III, c. 13.)] It is asked why there was not added here what was said of the animals of the waters: « And God blessed them, saying: Increase and multiply, » etc. Perhaps because what had been said of the first creature of a living soul, he left to be understood of the second, especially because in the works of this day he was about to say many other things. But herbs and trees, because they have not the affection of generating, he perhaps judged unworthy of this blessing. Which was of necessity repeated in man, lest anyone should think [0080A] that there is sin in the office of begetting sons, as in fornication without wedlock.

VERS. 25.-- « And God made the beasts of the earth, » etc. [n: (BEDA in Hexaem.)] It is a change of order. Nor need we care in our own speech which of the creatures we name first in order, since God created all things together.

[n: (ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.)] On the sixth day the earth brings forth the living soul, when our flesh, abstaining from dead works, brings forth living shoots of the virtues, according to its own kind, that is, by imitating the life of the saints. Whence I Cor. IV: « Be imitators of me, » etc. We live according to our own kind when we imitate the saints as our neighbours. The earth brings forth beasts, that is, men growing proud in ferocity. [0080B] And cattle, that is, those living simply. And serpents, namely the crafty, and those who discern good from evil, and who as it were by creeping search out earthly things, through which they may understand the heavenly.

VERS. 26.-- « Let us make man. » [n: (AUG., ibid., c. 19.)] There is intimated the plurality of the persons of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit, and straightway the unity of the deity, when it is said: « And God made man to the image of God, » not the Father to the image of the Son only. For it would not truly be said, to our image; but it was so said: « God made man to the image of God, » as if it were said « to his own image. » But when it is said « to the image of God, » whereas it was said above « to our image, » it is signified that the plurality of persons does not bring it about that [0080C] we should believe in many gods, but that we should receive the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit as one God.

« Let us make man. » [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem.)] It appears why it was said that the rest should be made, etc., *as far as* but that he might show that he had received back true flesh after death.

« And let him rule, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. III de Gen. c. 20.)] It is also to be noted that, when he had said « to our image, » he immediately subjoined: « And let him rule over the fishes of the sea and the fowls of the air. » That we may understand, namely, that man was made to the image of God in this, wherein he excels the irrational creatures. And that is reason, or mind, or understanding, or if it be more conveniently called by another name, that, namely, wherein he cleaves to the truth that is to be contemplated.

[0080D]VERS. 27.-- « And God created man to the image, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Gen., c. 1.)] He who, not by imitating any of the saints whatever, but by gazing upon truth itself, works justice, so that he may understand truth itself and follow it: he has received power over all things, because everyone made spiritual, and like to God, judges all things, and is himself judged by no man.

« And God created, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, cap. 20, 21, 22.)] It is to be noted that, as in the creating of light it is said: « Let there be light, » and straightway there follows, « and light was made; » so when it is said: « Let us make man, » there is brought in, « and God made man to the image of God: » because that nature is intellectual, as that light was: and this is for it to be made, namely [0081A] to acknowledge the Word through which it is made. etc., *as far as* but fore-sown materially.

« Male and female he created them, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem. )] Afterwards it is set forth more fully both whence and in what manner men were made, etc., *as far as* although virginity be preferred.

[n: (BEDA, ISID., ut supra. )] After these things « God made man to his own image and likeness, » a perfect man, namely, etc., *as far as* whence Paul: « We have sown unto you spiritual things, it is a great matter if we reap your carnal things [n: (I Cor. IX)]. » [var: Vulg. 1 Cor. 9:11 *Si nos vobis spiritualia seminavimus*, a conditional, "If we have sown unto you spiritual things", closing as a question]

VERS. 28.-- . . . . . « And rule over the fishes, » etc. What he had said before of them as it were by counselling, he now says commanding, so that man may have, namely, dominion over all the creatures in the lower part of the world. Which he follows out [0081B] one by one.

« And rule over the fishes of the sea. » [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem. )] It is asked with what usefulness man received dominion among the other living things, etc., *as far as* and that the gaping of beasts and the venom of serpents gave way.

VERS. 29.-- « And God said: Behold, I have given, » etc. [n: (BED., ibid.)] It is plain that before the sin of man the earth brought forth nothing harmful, no poisonous herb, no barren tree. For all the herbs and trees were given to men, and to the birds, and to the living things of the earth for food. Whence it is plain that then animals did not live by the eating of animals, but fed harmoniously on herbs and fruits.

VERS. 30.-- . . . . . « And it was so done. » [n: (AUG., de Gen. ad lit. lib. III, c. 13.)] It is to be noted that where power is given to man of eating herbs and fruits, there is subjoined, [0081C] « and it was so done: » wherein it is signified that, when God said this, man knew these things to be granted to him for food, not that he ate them at once. For if we refer to all the aforesaid things what he says, « and it was so done, » it will follow that we say they were already multiplied on the sixth day, which was done after many years.

VERS. 31.-- « And God saw, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid.)] It is asked: since the rest are severally called good, why is man, made to the image of God, said to be this along with the rest? But perhaps God foreknew that he would sin, and would not remain in the perfection of the image. For he who is good severally, is more so with all, but it is not convertible. It was therefore cautiously provided that there should be said what would be true in the present, and would signify the foreknowledge of the future. [0081D] But God ordered all things so that, if any severally should by transgressing be deformed, yet always with them the whole should be beautiful.

« And the evening and the morning were made, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen., tom. V, col. 207.)] The morning of the sixth day is the preaching of the Gospel through Christ, etc., *as far as* which has no evening.

*Morally. [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I in fine.)]* On the sixth day the earth brings forth the living soul, that is, man, out of the stability of his mind, where he has spiritual fruits, that is, good thoughts; he governs all the motions of his soul, so that there may be in him a living soul, that is, one serving reason and justice, not earthliness and sin. So man is made to the image and likeness [0082A] of God, male and female; that is, understanding and action: by whose coupling a spiritual offspring may fill the earth, that is, may subject the flesh, and the other things which have already been said in the perfection of man. For in these days the evening is the very perfecting of the several works, and the morning the beginning of those that follow.



## CHAPTER II.

VERS. 1.-- « Therefore the heavens and the earth were finished, » etc. [n: (ALCUIN., in Gen. tom. I, col. 517.)] In no way can it be told how God made heaven and earth. But this exposition, by the order of the days, sets forth as it were a history of things done, yet above all it attends to the foretelling of things to come, etc., *as far as* which the Lord showed to lie hidden to good purpose.

[0082B] « Therefore the heavens were finished, and the earth, and all the furniture of them. » *[n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. I, c. ult.)] Allegorically.* After those works of, as it were, six days, works very good, let man hope for perpetual rest, and let him understand what it is, » and God rested on the seventh day from his works: » because he himself also works these good things in us, who commands that we should work. And he is rightly said to rest: because after all these works he will furnish us with rest, in the way that the master of a household builds a house, when he commands his servants to do it; and afterwards to rest from his works, when, the building finished, he bids them rest.

[n: (AUG., ibid.)] Each one of us has in his works and in an upright life these six days, as it were, marked off, after which he ought to hope for quiet: on the first day [0082C] the light of faith, when first he believes things visible, for which faith the Lord vouchsafed to appear visibly. On the second day there was made as it were a firmament of discipline, which distinguishes between carnal and spiritual works, as the firmament between the lower waters and the higher. On the third day, on which he prepares and raises up his mind to bring forth the fruits of good works, the stain and the waves of carnal temptations being set apart, he has his mind dry, as it were, from the disturbances of the sea, so that he can now say: « With the mind I serve the law of God [n: (Rom. VII)], » etc.

VERS. 2, 3.-- « And God finished, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem. )] Another version: « God completed on the sixth day the works which he made, » which brings no question with it, because the things that were done in it are manifest, etc., [0082D] *as far as* for Solomon did some work when he dedicated the temple.

[n: (HIER., Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III, col. 935.)] In the Hebrew it stands « on the seventh day. » We shall therefore press the Jews hard, who glory in the leisure of the sabbath, because already then, in the beginning, the sabbath was loosed, while God works on the sabbath, completing his works on it.

« And God finished, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. IV de Gen. ad litt., c. 4, tom. III.)] It is asked whether, etc., *as far as* according to him they would not be made perfect.

« And God rested, etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem. )] Not as though wearied by working, but he rested from the whole work, because he ceased to make a new creature. For to rest is said to be to cease, as in Apocalypse IV: [0083A] « They had no rest, saying: Holy, holy, holy, » that is, they did not cease to say it.

[n: (BEDA, ibid.)] More deeply too he will be understood to have rested from his whole work, etc., *as far as* but he gave rest in himself to rational creatures.

« And God rested, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 8.)] God did not labour in working, who made by a word alone, saying: « Let it be made. » Nor does a man labour by saying, « Let it be made. » But perhaps he is said to have laboured in thinking what should be made. From which care, things being finished, he is said to have rested. But to be wise in this fashion is to be witless. That God rested, therefore, is that he furnished rest in himself to the rational creature; that is, that we may be borne by desire thither, where we may rest, that is, may seek nothing further. For as he is said to make what we make by his working in us, [0083B] and to know when we know, so to rest, when by his gift we rest.

[n: (Ibid., c. 11.)] It is no wonder if, foretelling in this manner the day on which Christ was to rest in the sepulchre, he said: « On the seventh day he rested, » being thereafter about to work the orders of the ages.

[n: (Ibid., c. 12.)] But it can be understood that God rested from making the kinds of creature, etc., *as far as* and does not cease to govern.

[n: (Ibid., c. 14.)] But God was neither wearied by creating nor refreshed by ceasing: rather through his Scripture he exhorts us to the desire of rest, by saying that he sanctified the day on which he rested. For he is not read to have sanctified the other days of his work, as though with him rest avails more than working.

[0083C] [n: (Ibid., c. 15.)] It is also a vice of the soul so to delight in its own works, etc., *as far as* by that rest from which he never withdrew.

[n: (Ibid.)] But if he were said to have rested from things to be made, etc., *as far as* but by him we are blessed.

[n: (Ibid.)] But in himself God has always rested: yet in the days in which the completing of things is recounted, he rested on the seventh only, which follows the perfection of things; for he rests from things perfected, who has no need of things perfected that he may be more blessed.

[n: (Ibid., c. 17.)] His works we see to be good: but his rest we shall see after our good works. To signify which, he commanded one day of the sabbath to be observed: which in the time of grace has been taken away, in [0083D] which he keeps a perpetual sabbath who works all good things in hope of what is to come, and does not glory in his good works as though he had not received them.

[n: (Ibid., c. 18.)] With him there is to his rest neither morning nor evening, because it is neither opened by a beginning nor closed by an end. But in works perfected it has a morning, and not an evening; because the perfected creature begins to be turned to the quiet of the Creator, but that quiet has no end of its perfection, and so the rest of God is begun not for God himself, but for the perfection of things, and it has a morning in that which is perfected by him: but in its own kind it is ended as by an evening, which in God has no evening, because nothing will be more perfect than perfection itself.

[0084A] [n: (Ibid.)] But in those days, etc., *as far as* which are recorded as created before the luminaries.

[n: (Ibid., c. 20.)] It is asked how it is said on the seventh, etc., *as far as* except that which was created first and called day.

[n: (Ibid., c. 21.)] But how could the light go round, etc., *as far as* and the rest.

[n: (Ibid.)] But it is easier that we confess ourselves ignorant, etc., *as far as* and it will be false that he completed all his works on the sixth day.

[n: (Ibid., c. 22.)] But since bodily light, etc., *as far as* and that by which the creature is known in itself is rightly called evening.

[n: (Ibid., c. 25.)] But because the angels so know the creature in itself, etc., *as far as* Therefore on the seventh day [0084B] no evening came on.

[n: (Ibid., c. 28.)] Nor let anyone think, etc., *as far as* and more truly morning?

[n: (Ibid., c. 35.)] That first day, therefore, etc., *as far as* and for this it deserved to be sanctified.

*[n: (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. II, col. 13.)] Allegorically.* Whence it is said in Exod. XX: « Remember that thou keep holy the sabbath day. » But this blessing and sanctification signifies a greater blessing and sanctification, etc., *as far as* Therefore this day is not written to have an evening.

VERS. 4.-- « These are the generations. » [n: (RAB.)] He touches those who say that the world always was without a beginning, or who think that it was made by God, but out of matter which he did not make, but which is coeternal with him.

[0084C] « In the day that he made, » etc. He puts the day for the whole time in which the primordial creature was formed, etc., *as far as* without rain and human labour.

« These are the generations of the heaven, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen., t. V.)] There are numbered six days in which the whole of the age from beginning to end, etc., *as far as* if he had not sinned.

« Before it should spring up in the earth. » That is, before he sinned, that is, before he made himself subject to earthly desires. Whence there follows: « For God had not rained upon the earth, » that is, he had not yet, by prophetic or evangelical clouds, the shower of the word being sent forth, made the soul to live, and « there was no man to till the earth, » because after sin man began to labour on the earth, [0084D] he had need of those clouds, whence the shrub, that is the soul, grew green. The fountain of life watered it, that is, the flood of truth, speaking in his understanding, so that it needed no rain from the clouds before he sinned. This was the state of man before sin.

[n: (AUG., l. V, de Genes. ad lit., c. 1, tom. III.)] Another version has: « This is the book of the creation of heaven and earth, » etc., *as far as* it nourishes the great with truth, the little ones with affability.

[n: (AUG., ibid., l. VI, c. 10.)] In the first founding of the world, etc., *as far as* seed from the herb.

VERS. 5.-- « And every shrub, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid., l. V, c. 4.)] The earth is understood to have brought forth herb and tree causally, that is, to have received the power of bringing forth. For in it, as in roots, had been made [0085A] the things that were to be through the times to come. He made them, therefore, before they were upon the earth, according to the formability of matter — the things that were to be formed by his word — preceding the forming not in time but in origin.

« For the Lord had not rained, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid., c. 5.)] As though God did not then make in the way he now makes, etc., *as far as* nor could the creature be moved.

VERS. 6.-- « But a fountain, » etc. [n: (RAB.)] By the watering of this fountain, of whatever kind it was, the earth came on already clothed with herbs and trees. One fountain is said to have gone up, for some unity within the bounds of the earth; or the singular is put for the plural.

« But a fountain, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Gen., c. 2.)] Here he seems to intimate what things come to be according to the intervals of times, etc., *as far as* afterwards, through these things that are known, [0085B] to be known in whatever measure.

VERS. 7.-- « He formed, » etc. [n: (AUG.)] *Mystically.* A broader narrative concerning man is unfolded figuratively, etc., *as far as* that he neither despise what he is, nor claim what he is not.

« The Lord God therefore formed man, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. lib. III, cap. 20 et 25.)] First out of the slime of the earth was formed an animal body, etc., *as far as* for it has the necessity of dying.

[n: (Ibid. c. 27.)] The first robe is either justice, from which he fell: or, if it signifies bodily immortality, this he lost, since by sinning he could not attain to it. So then Adam had an animal body, not only before paradise, but in paradise as well (although in the inner man he was spiritual), which he lost by sinning, [0085C] and deserved the death of the body — he who by not sinning would have deserved the change into a spiritual body.

« He formed, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. VI de Gen. ad litt., c. I, tom. III.)] It must be seen whether it be a recapitulation, so that it is told how man was made, whom we read to have been made on the sixth day. Or whether then, when he made all things together, he made man also among them, so that with the accession of time he should be made in this manner too, in which he leads his life in this visible form? just as the hay, made before it sprang up, with time coming on and with the watering of the fountain, sprang up, so as to be upon the earth.



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

[n: (AUG., ibid. c. 2, 3.)] Let us look first according to recapitulation, etc., *as far as* but then invisibly, potentially and causally, in the manner in which things to come, not things done, come to be.

[0085D] « He formed, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem. )] The making of man, etc., *as far as* he created that in which he should live.

[n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., l. VI, c. 13 et 17.)] It is asked whether God made man suddenly in the age of manhood, etc., *as far as* and therefore it was of necessity to come.

And he breathed into his face the breath of life, and man was made into a living soul. » [n: (AUG., ibid., l. VII, c. 3, 5, 7.)] Without doubt, « he blew, or he breathed upon, » is what must be said, etc., *as far as* a punishment if he should despise it?

[n: (Ibid., c. 26.)] It must be said, etc., *as far as* it is not lawful to doubt.

[n: (Ibid., c. 27.)] It is commonly asked whether, if it be unwilling to be embodied, it is compelled, etc., *as far as* he made them.

[0086A] [n: (Ibid., l. VI, c. 7.)] But perhaps to the soul made on the sixth day, where the image of God shines back, etc., *as far as* which is to be preferred in matters of this kind.

[n: (IRENAEUS.)] The habit of life which makes a man animal is one thing; the life-giving Spirit, which works a spiritual man, is another. Whence Isaias: Who gave breath to the peoples upon the earth, and spirit to them that tread upon it; so that God gave breath indeed to every people that is upon the earth, but spirit only to those who tread down earthly desires: so that the breath is for a time, but the spirit eternal, inasmuch as it is properly the gift of God.

VERS. 8.-- « And he had planted, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III.)] From that beginning he planted paradise, etc., *as far as* and because he was about to speak specially of the tree of life and of the knowledge [0086B] of good and evil.

« The Lord planted a paradise in Eden toward the East, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. ad litt., lib. VIII, c. 1.)] There are three general opinions concerning paradise. One is of those who will have it understood only bodily, another of those who will have it only spiritually, but a third of those who take paradise in both ways. I confess that the third pleases me: so that the man made of slime, which is the human body, is understood to be placed in a bodily paradise. Adam, although he signifies something else, because he is the figure of him that was to come, is taken in his own nature as a man.

[n: (Ibid.)] « And the Lord planted a paradise in Eden. » That is, in delights. Then he recapitulates this, to show how God planted paradise. « And God sent forth out of the earth every tree, » etc. For already [0086C] then the earth had brought forth every tree, namely on the third day. But since out of these kinds the trees were established in paradise, he brought forth openly, and in its own time, what the earth had already brought forth causally on the third day.

[n: (STRAB.)] Certain codices have: « Eden toward the Rising. » From which we can conjecture that paradise is set in the East. But wherever it be, we know that it is earthly, and, with the Ocean set between and mountains standing over against it, most remote from our world, set on a height, reaching as far as the lunar circle; whence the waters of the flood by no means came thither.

« In which he placed him. » [n: (AUG., ubi supra, cap. 5.)] If anyone thinks that souls stripped of the body are contained in bodily places, etc., *as far as* by one cake of which God freed a man from hunger for forty days.

[0086D]VERS. 9.-- « And the Lord brought forth the tree of life also. » [n: (AUG., Ibid., c. 4.)] Of wisdom it is said: « It is a tree of life to them that lay hold on her. » But there is an eternal Jerusalem in the heavens, etc., *as far as* which narrated things done in their own times.

« The tree of life also, » etc. [n: (BEDA, in Hexaem., tom. III, col. 13.)] In the one there was for man a sign of the obedience which he owed: in the other a sacrament of eternal life, which by obeying he would merit. It was called the tree of life, because it received from God that whoever ate of it, his body should be strengthened with a stable soundness, nor should it slip by any infirmity or age into the worse or into decline.

[n: (STRAB.)] *Historically.* The tree of life had naturally this [0087A] power, that whoever ate of its fruit should be clothed with perpetual solidity, to be wearied by no infirmity, or anxiety, or weariness of old age, or feebleness.

« And the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. » [n: (AUG. ubi supra, c. 6)] It was bodily like the other trees, and not harmful as food, etc., *as far as* which followed the transgression.

« The tree of the knowledge of good and evil. » [n: (AUG., lib. II de Gen. contra Manich., c. 10.)] The mean of the soul and its ordered integrity is signified, in that it was planted in the midst of paradise, and called of the knowledge of good and evil: because the soul, which ought to be stretched out toward God, if, God being forsaken, it be turned to itself, and will enjoy its own power without God, then, punishment following, learns by experience what [0087B] lies between the good which it forsook and the evil into which it fell: and this will be for it to have tasted of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil.

VERS. 10, 11, 12, 13, 14.-- « And a river went out. » [n: (BED., ubi supra.)] To water paradise, etc., *as far as* and, emerging a little after, to hold their accustomed course.

[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 7.)] Another version: « A fountain went out of Eden, which watereth paradise, » etc. These rivers to the nations, etc., *as far as* namely trees fair and fruitful.

[n: (ISID., AUG.)] *Mystically.* The river going out of paradise is the affluence of eternal gladness, etc., *as far as* by bearing adversities.

[n: (GREG.)] *Morally.* By four rivers going out of paradise [0087C] the earth is watered. Prudence, temperance, fortitude, justice hold together the solid building of our reason: because by these four virtues the whole structure of good work rises up. Four rivers water paradise, because while the heart is flooded with these four virtues, it is tempered from every heat of carnal desires.

« Evilath. » A region of India which after the flood was possessed by Evila, the son of Jectan, the son of Heber, the patriarch of the Hebrews. Evilath is interpreted *bringing to birth,* because one who speaks the truth is more in travail than he brings forth. Pliny says that the regions of India abound in veins of gold beyond all others.

« Bdellium. » According to Pliny it is an aromatic tree of the size of an olive, whose gum is clear, [0087D] bitter to the taste, of a good smell, but more fragrant with an infusion of wine.

« Onyx. » It is a precious stone, so called because it has a whiteness mixed in it after the likeness of a human nail, because a nail is called in Greek ὄνυξ. The ancient version has « carbuncle and prase. » The carbuncle is of a fiery colour, and is said to light up the darkness of night. The prase is green, whence it took its name in Greek from the leek, which is called πράσον.

VERS. 15.-- « The Lord God therefore took, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 8.)] He has already said that man was placed in paradise. Now he recapitulates to what end he was placed there, « that he might dress it, » etc. For it is not credible that God condemned him to labour before sin, [0088A] etc., *as far as* whence Eccli. X: « for the beginning of man's pride is to fall away from God. »

« The Lord God therefore took the man, » etc. [n: (GREG., lib. XIX Moral., cap. 17, tom. I.)] It must be weighed that good things cannot profit, if the evils that creep in are not guarded against, etc., *as far as* but by growing proud under them they sink beneath them.

« And the Lord God took, » etc. [n: (HIERON. Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III.)] For « pleasure » the Hebrew has « Eden. » The Seventy themselves, therefore, have rendered it now « Eden, » now « pleasure. » But Symmachus, who « had before translated it a flowering paradise, here put « pleasantness, » or « delights. »

« And he put him, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] *Mystically.* From that which is added, « And that he should keep it, it is hinted what kind of work [0088B] it was: because in the tranquillity of the blessed life, where death is not, all work is to keep what thou holdest. For a precept was given, that they should take of every tree of paradise, but not of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil: that is, that they should not so enjoy it as to violate the integrity of their nature by usurping what was forbidden.

VERS. 16.-- « And he enjoineth him, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 13.)] He was forbidden from the tree, etc., *as far as* what lies between the good of obedience and the evil of disobedience.

[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 17.)] It is asked whether to the man alone, or to the woman also, etc., *as far as* « let them ask their husbands at home. »

[n: (PROCOP. in Gen. )] Some say: If he willed to clothe us with immortality, what need was there of a precept, on account of [0088C] which we were to sin and then to feel the severe stings of his judgment? I answer: Since he is kind and gentle, and willed to lay claim to the human race for an incorruptible and immortal condition: lest they should set that benefit down to their own or proper power, he acted in this manner, he willed that they should render him thanks, and for them he also appointed the present life as a wrestling-school and, as it were, a schooling for the life to come. Then, bringing in death on account of sin, he willed that we should acknowledge our infirmity, and that thereafter we ourselves should the more desire the gift to come.

« And he commanded him, saying: Of every, etc. [n: (AUG. de Gen. ad litt., l. VIII, 28, tom. III.)] If it be asked how he spoke these things, it cannot properly be comprehended by us. For it is certain that he speaks either through his own substance, [0088D] etc., *as far as* but through a creature subject to himself.

It is asked: how could they speak, or understand one speaking, who had not learned by growing up among speakers, or by teaching? But God had made them such as could speak, and could learn from others if there were any.

[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 14.)] Of Christ it is said, Isa. VII: « Before the child knows good or evil, he shall despise wickedness, that he may choose the good. » How does he either despise or choose what he does not know? etc., *as far as* what he ought not to have lost.

VERS. 17.-- « But of the tree, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 15.)] The tree from this, etc., *as far as* that is, that they were not about to lose what they loved.

[n: (SEVERIANUS.)] There were three differences of trees in [0089A] paradise. One indeed had been given him that he might live, another that he might live well, the third that he might live always.



## CHAPTER II. (cont.)

« For in whatsoever day, » etc. He does not say, If thou eat, thou shalt be mortal, but « thou shalt die the death. » For man died in his soul when he sinned, because God withdrew from him, who is the life of the soul: upon which followed the death of the body, the soul departing from him, which is the life of the body; and this befell Adam when he ended the present life. It can be understood thus, that when they sinned, they were at once punished with that death of which it is said in Rom. seven: « Unhappy man that I am! who shall deliver me from the body of this death? » and in the same, chapter eight: « The body is dead because of sin; » he does not say mortal, but « dead, » although it is mortal, because it is to die. It is not to be believed that before sin those bodies were so, [0089B] but animal, not yet spiritual, yet not dead — bodies, that is, which must needs die, which came about on the day of the transgression.

« Thou shalt die the death. » A twofold death is signified: that of the soul, the Lord departing, who is its life; or that of the body, which he received at the end of his life. But at once he incurred the necessity of dying.

VERS. 18.-- « The Lord also said, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. IX de Gen., c. 8.)] Those who hold that it was not lawful to have intercourse in paradise for the begetting of children, etc., *as far as* soul from soul, or in whatever other way the soul comes to be.

VERS. 19.-- « He brought them, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. IX de Gen. ad litt., c. 14, tom. III.)] Not as hunters do, or fowlers; nor was there a voice of command made out of a cloud, such as rational souls are wont to understand; for this [0089C] beasts or birds do not naturally understand, etc., *as far as* they are understood to have been created of earth.

[n: (HIERON.)] It seems that the first language of the human race was Hebrew, because the names which we read in Genesis until the division of tongues are agreed to be of that language.

VERS. 20.-- « And he called, » etc. [n: (AUG.)] *Mystically.* Signifying the Gentiles who should be saved in the Church through Christ, and should receive the name of Christ, which before they did not have; whence, Isa. LXV: « I will call my servants by another name. »

« But for Adam there was not found, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. IX de Gen. ad litt., cap. 15 et seq.)] It must be seen how the forming of the woman was made, which is mystically called a building, etc., *as far as* that we may understand that through that ecstasy Adam said these things by God's gift.

[0089D] « But for Adam there was not found a helper. » No faithful just man can be made equal to Christ. Psalm XLIV: « For he is beautiful in form above the sons of men. » For no one could deliver the human race but himself. Whence Apocalypse V: « No one was found worthy to open the book and to loose the seals thereof, » etc.

« But for Adam there was not found, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 13.)] The woman was made for the help of the man, etc., *as far as* and to subjugate to the dominion of reason, grace cooperating.

VERS. 21.-- « The Lord therefore cast, » etc. *[n: (ISID. ex Aug., ut supra, c. 13, 14.)] Mystically.* These things cannot be seen with bodily eyes. But the further a man withdraws from things visible to the secrets of understanding, as it were by falling asleep, [0090A] the better and more sincerely he sees, etc., *as far as* and to be referred to the Church.

[n: (AUG., lib. X de Gen. ad litt., c. I.)] Those who think that the soul is from a soul, etc., *as far as* which had been said of the man.

[n: (AUG., ibid., c. 19, 20.)] If it be asked whence Christ received his soul, I would rather hear men better and more learned; etc., *as far as* to whom what answer might be made has not yet occurred to me.

« The Lord therefore cast a deep sleep, » etc. [n: (GREG., Moral., l. XXX, tom. II.)] We do not attain the summit of contemplation, if we do not cease from the oppression of outward care: nor do we look upon our own selves, so as to know that there is in us one thing rational which rules, another animal which is ruled, unless, betaking ourselves to the secret of silence, [0090B] we are lulled asleep from outward disturbance. Which Adam well figured in his sleeping, from whose side the woman presently came forth. For he who is caught up to the understanding of inward things closes his eyes to visible things, and then understands that there is in himself one thing which ought to rule, as a man; another which should be ruled, as a woman.

VERS. 22, 24.-- « And he built. . . . Wherefore a man shall leave, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen., t. V.)] What was fulfilled in Adam according to the history, etc., *as far as* For the serpent prefigures the poisons of heretics.

VERS. 25.-- « And they were both naked. » [n: (AUG.)] If anything is said in the words of God or of any prophet whatever which seems absurd to the letter and therefore to have been said figuratively, yet it is not to be doubted that it was said for the sake of its signification. But the bodies of the two human beings [0090C] in paradise were naked, and they were not ashamed, because they had felt no law in their members warring against the law of the mind, which followed upon disobedience. They thought nothing was to be veiled, because they had felt nothing was to be restrained.

[n: (AUG., de Civ. Dei, l. XIV, c. 17, tom. VII, col. 425.)] Deservedly is there shame chiefly of lust, etc., *as far as* and it rendered them confounded.

[n: (RUPERT.)] That they were naked was not a matter of ignominy, but of honour: that they were not ashamed of their nakedness was not a matter of folly, but of security. For what? did it receive this from its maker, that the workmanship of God is shameful and to be blushed at? Or is it nature, and not rather conscience, that makes confusion of this kind? It is indeed as it were turned into nature, [0090D] this torment of confusion: nevertheless its origin sprouted not from the primeval condition, but from a later fault. For we blush at our nakedness for this reason, that we are conscious to ourselves how great an infirmity, nay how great a rebellion of our own flesh against us, we carry about. But then the conscience of the heart was sound, since no concupiscence of the eyes solicited it; seeing that the concupiscence of the flesh is the punishment of sin, by which the pride of the mind that went before is punished. God, says the Scripture, made man upright. But the uprightness of man lies in this, that the spirit should preside over and command the flesh, as the superior over the inferior: for the rational spirit of man was set midway between God and his own flesh, that it might obey God [0091A] and command the flesh. And because that former man disturbed and broke this order, by casting away the precept of God: the spirit, which was superior, was made subject to the lowest flesh, so that by a just punishment he should lie beneath the inferior, who scorned rightly to be beneath the superior. Hence that confusion of the face, breaking out from the secrets of conscience to public eyes, and driving a penal ignominy to go beneath the wretched solaces of coverings. This infirmity, I say, or rebellion of the flesh, was not yet: rightly therefore was each of them naked and they were not ashamed, because they had nothing at which to blush. That security of nakedness being lost, we groan, in that we are despoiled of glory and honour and wrapped about with rags. For so clothed we truly walk naked, even if — which is impossible — we should be clothed as the lilies [0091B] of the field are clothed. For not even Solomon in all his glory was clothed as one of these. If, I say, we could be clothed as these are clothed, even so are we despoiled and naked. Whence we look for the Saviour, our Lord Jesus Christ, who will reform the body of our lowliness, made like to the body of his brightness. For when the just shall shine as the sun in the kingdom of their Father, shall they be covered with tunics and cloaks? By no means, but to the glory of their maker, whatever vessels of mercy they shall have been, they will lie open to one another with mutual sights of glory, shining without, and joyful within; whole in body, and glad in mind; blessed, I say, in body and soul alike.
