« And they were both naked, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen., ex Aug. de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 15.)] That [0091C] they were naked and were ashamed, etc., *as far as* because in consent the conscience is held guilty.



## CHAPTER III.

VERS. 1-4.-- « But the serpent also was more subtle than all the living creatures, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt. c. 2, 3, tom. III.)] Others, « most prudent, » said by transference. Certain codices have « most wise, » not in the proper sense, as the wisdom of God, or of the angels, or of the rational soul, is wont to be taken in a good sense, etc., *as far as* but the power is from God.

« But the serpent, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] So the devil spoke through the serpent, which knew nothing, as he does through the possessed or the frenzied: for whomsoever he seizes, he takes away understanding and reason. But the good Spirit, whomsoever he has filled with grace, makes them seeing and understanding, [0091D] whence the spirit of the prophets is subject to the prophets.

« Who said to the woman. » [n: (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 30, et ex capp. 7, 8, 6, 10, passim.)] The transgression of the woman is inexcusable, because she was mindful of God's precept. Yet forgetfulness, etc., *as far as these* c. 10: He foresees that there will be good men and evil, and creates them: furnishing himself to the good for their enjoyment, bestowing many things even upon the evil.

« Why hath he commanded, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Therefore the crafty enemy asks this, and calls to mind God's command, lest, when she had sinned, she should have an excuse as though she had forgotten it.

« Why hath he commanded, » etc. [n: (GREG. Moral., l. XXIII, tom. II.)] The enemy of the human race does the same daily: he labours to tear the words [0092A] of God out of the hearts of men, and to make his threats light, and to bring them to believe what he falsely promises, for he invites them to temporal things, that he may make light of the eternal punishments which God threatens. For, pledging the glory of the present life, he says: « Taste, and you shall be as gods, » and touch temporal concupiscence, and appear exalted in this world; and when he tries to remove the fear of the divine sentence, he says as it were: « Why hath God commanded you? »

[n: (AUG., lib. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 4, tom. III.)] It is asked why God permitted man to be tempted, etc., *as far as* or earthly, as is the soul of man.

[n: (Ibid., c. 5.)] Nor is it to be thought that man would have been cast down, unless there had gone before in him a certain elation to be repressed, so that through the humbling of sin he might know that he had falsely [0092B] presumed concerning himself, and that a nature once made is not well with itself, if it has withdrawn from its maker. For it is commended what a good God is, when it is well for no one to withdraw from him.

[n: (Ibid., c. 18.)] This question concerning the blessed life, etc., *as far as* less than in the life of the holy angels.

[n: (Ibid., c. 16.)] It can be thought not without cause that the devil fell from the beginning of time, etc., *as far as* to whom God did not give foreknowledge concerning themselves?

[n: (Ibid., c. 14.)] But how can they be blessed, to whom their own blessedness is uncertain? etc., *as far as* but on what ground they assert it does not occur to me.

[n: (Ibid., c. 21.)] But it must be thought not without cause that from the very beginning of the creature, etc., *as far as* foreseeing [0092C] how great things he would do out of him in his own goodness.

[n: (Ibid., c. 22.)] « But the beginning of the thing formed » is said, etc., *as far as* as evil men.

[n: (Ibid., c. 23.)] Whereas, then, it is thought that the devil never stood in the truth, etc., *as far as* more things agree with his body.

[n: (GREG., hom. 16, in Evang., tom. II.)] The devil tempted the first parent in three ways, etc., *as far as* by delight we are overcome, by consent we are bound.

VERS. 5.--. . . . . « You shall be as gods, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] *Mystically.* He frames his words with artful guile, so that, if he cannot overthrow them by contempt of obedience, at least in this, that he has corrupted faith and persuaded to idolatry, he may boast himself the victor: but if he has seduced them by contempt of obedience, he may stand [0092D] victor in both.

[n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 15, tom. III.)] The woman, being questioned, answered, etc., *as far as* for, with none ruling him, God alone is blessed through his own power.

VERS. 6, 7.-- « She saw therefore, » etc. [n: (ISID. ex Aug. ubi supra. )] How did they see, if their eyes were closed? But it was said so that we may understand that those eyes were opened, after they had eaten of the fruit, with which they saw themselves naked and were displeased: that is, the eyes of craftiness, to which simplicity is displeasing. For when a man has fallen from the inmost light of truth, there is nothing whereby pride would please, save by fraudulent pretences. For hence is born hypocrisy, in which those seem very [0093A] shrewd who have been able to deceive whom they would.

« She saw therefore, » etc. [n: (GREG., lib. XXI Moral., cap. 2.)] Lest we be turned about in slippery thought, we ought not to gaze upon what it is not lawful to desire, etc., *as far as* the discipline of the outward senses also must be kept.

« And she gave to her husband, who did eat. And the eyes of them both were opened, » etc. [n: (ISID. ex Aug. ubi supra, c. 16, 17.)] Of whom it was said: « Then they saw that they were naked, » [var: Vulg. Gn 3:7 *cumque cognovissent se esse nudos*, "and when they had known that they were naked"] with perverse eyes, namely, to which nakedness, that is simplicity, seemed a thing to blush at. etc., *as far as* « My soul is troubled within myself. » [n: (Psal. XLI.)]

« Who did eat, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen., lib. XI, c. 3, tom. III, col. 429.)] Namely to lust [0093B] after one another, etc., *as far as* which would convict the sinner by the deed, and teach the reader by the writing.

« They sewed together fig leaves. » [n: (ALCUIN., in Gen. t. I.)] Because they had lost the glory of simple chastity, they fled for refuge to the twofold itch of lust; whence John I: « When thou wast under the fig tree I saw thee, and I came down, » [var: Vulg. Jo 1:48 *cum esses sub ficu, vidi te*, with no *et descendi*] supply « to deliver thee. »

VERS. 8.-- « And when they had heard, » etc. [n: (AUG., l. II de Gen. ad litt., c. 33.)] For at that hour it was fitting that such as they should be visited, because they had fallen away from the light of truth, etc., *as far as* he may be believed to have appeared to the bodily senses by a local and temporal motion.

« He hid himself, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid.)] When God turns away his face within and man is troubled, things like madness come about, through excessive fear and shame, with a hidden [0093C] instinct also not resting: so that unknowing they did things which should signify something to those who came after, for whose sake these things are written.

« At the afternoon air. » Whence at the ninth hour Christ gave up the spirit [n: (Matth. XXVII)]. He was crucified at the sixth hour, that man might be restored at the same hour at which he was cast out of paradise.

« In the midst of the tree of paradise. » He hides himself who, turned away from the precept of the Creator, lives by the will of his own error and choice.

VERS. 9.-- « And the Lord God called Adam. » [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 34.)] By rebuking, that is, not by being ignorant, etc., *as far as* but now we are not reporting things signified, but defending things done.

VERS. 10-11.-- « And he said: I heard thy voice, » etc. [0093D] *[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 34.)] Historically.* It is probable that a human form was wont to appear to the first men through a fitting creature, etc., *as far as* and there followed that of which they were ashamed.

« Thy voice, » etc. *[n: (ISID., in Gen. tom. V.)] Mystically.* He answers that, having heard his voice, he had hidden himself, etc., *as far as* and not to obey the precept of God.

« And God called Adam, » etc. [n: (GREG.)] Because he saw him fallen into fault, now under sin, as though hidden from the eyes of truth, because he does not approve the darkness of his error, he is as it were ignorant where the sinner is, and calls him and seeks him, saying: « Adam, where art thou? » [var: Vulg. Gn 3:9 *et dixit ei: Ubi es?*, without the vocative *Adam*] While he calls, he signifies that he is calling him back to penance; while he seeks him, [0094A] he intimates that he knows not sinners justly condemned.

VERS. 12.-- « And Adam said: The woman whom thou gavest, » etc. [n: (AUG.)] He does not say, I have sinned: for pride has the deformity of confusion, not the humility of confession. But it was written for this, and the questions were put for this, that they might be written truly and profitably, so that we may know with how great a disease of pride they labour who today try to refer evil to the Creator, and, if they do any good, to attribute it to themselves.

« The woman whom thou gavest, » etc. As though she had been given for this, that she herself should not obey the man, and both of them God.

« The woman whom thou gavest, » etc. [n: (GREG., lib. IV Mor., c. 27, tom. I, col. 661.)] In four ways is sin committed in the heart, etc., *as far as* at the last it is nourished by the seduction of false hope, or by the obstinacy of wretched despair [0094B].

VERS. 13.-- « And the Lord God said to the woman, » etc. [n: (AUG.)] Neither does she confess the sin, but refers it to another — in an unequal sex, with equal haughtiness. Yet David, born of these, says unto the end of the world: « I said: Lord, be merciful to me, heal my soul, for I have sinned against thee [n: (Psal. XL)]. » This ought to have been said by those two: but God does not yet regard the necks of sinners: there remained the labours and sorrows of death, and the wearing away of the world, and the grace of God, which comes to help in due time, teaching the afflicted not to presume of themselves.

« The serpent deceived, » etc. As though anyone's persuasion should prevail over God's precept.

« The serpent deceived me, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid., lib. II, c. 30.)] How by these words, etc., *as far as* since with that [0094C] food he did not see her to be dead.

[n: (AUG., ibid., c. 28.)] It is to be noted that the devil was not permitted to tempt the woman, etc., *as far as* but reason shows that on this account he envied man.

[n: (ALC.)] The sin of the angel was left unspoken, that of man laid open, because the one was incurable, the other was cured.

« She saw therefore, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] She is plainly shown to be inexcusable, since it was open to her, under such deliberation, to mark the wiles of the enemy.

VERS. 14.-- « And the Lord God said, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 36.)] This whole sentence is figurative. Where nevertheless it must be believed that all things were said. The serpent is not asked why he did it, because it was not he that did it by his own will and nature, but the devil out of him, and through him, and in him: who already for his impiety and pride had been destined [0094D] for eternal fire. To him, therefore, is referred what is said to the serpent, and it is shown what he will be to the human race.

*Mystically.* « And the Lord God said to the serpent, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. cont. Manich., lib. II, c. 17.)] The serpent, unquestioned, is the first to receive punishment, etc., *as far as* that he may shut out the very beginning of the evil suggestion.

« Upon thy breast, » etc. [n: (HIERON.)] « Belly » the Seventy added, but in the Hebrew there is « breast [nt: In the Hebrew it is *gahon*, which signifies *belly* and *breast*.] » only, that he might lay open the craft and cunning of his thoughts, that is, that his every step is of wickedness and of fraud.

[0095A] « With breast and belly thou shalt creep. » [n: (GREG., lib. XXI Mor., c. 2.)] The evil of lechery is committed either in thought or in deed. For when the enemy is driven out from the accomplishment of the deed, he labours to defile the secret places of thought. He creeps with the belly, when, slippery, through the human members made subject to him, he practises lechery in deed. He crawls with the breast, when he defiles in thought. But because it is through thought that one comes to deeds, he is described as creeping first with the breast and afterwards with the belly.



## CHAPTER III. (cont.)

VERS. 15.-- « I will put enmities between thee and the woman . . . She shall crush thy head, » etc. [n: (GREG. l. I Mor., c. 38.)] To crush the serpent's head is to root out the beginnings of his suggestion from the entrance of the heart by the hand of watchful consideration; for when he is caught at the very outset, he contrives to strike the heel, because the man whom he does not strike in the first temptation [0095B] he aims to deceive at the end. But if the heart is corrupted in the temptation, the middle and the end of the action that follows are possessed by the enemy, because he sees the whole tree bearing fruit for him, the tree which he has vitiated at the root with the tooth of his venom.

[n: (HIERON.)] « She shall keep thy heel, and thou shalt crush her heel. » [var: Vulg. Gn 3:15 *ipsa conteret caput tuum, et tu insidiaberis calcaneo ejus*, "she shall crush thy head, and thou shalt lie in wait for her heel"] It stands better in the Hebrew: « She shall crush thy head, and thou shalt crush her heel, » because both our steps are hampered by the serpent, and the Lord shall crush Satan under her feet.

VERS. 16.-- « To the woman also, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 37.)] These things are better understood figuratively and prophetically, etc., *as far as* nature will be depraved, and [0095C] guilt increased.

« I will multiply thy sorrows, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 19.)] It is manifest that the woman's pains were multiplied, etc., *as far as* but first there was a struggling with grief against an evil habit.

VERS. 17-18.-- . . . « Cursed is the earth, » etc. [n: (AUG. ibid., c. 38.)] That these are the labors of the human race upon earth no one is ignorant, and that they would not be, if the happiness which was in paradise were held fast, stands beyond doubt.

[n: (STRAB.)] The earth is cursed, not Adam, so that all men for whose sake these things were written might be terrified, lest, doing the like, they should be struck with a like punishment. But Cain himself, because he added fratricide to the first transgression, is cursed when it is said: « Thou shalt be cursed upon the earth, » [0095D] etc.

[n: (AUG.)] He cursed the earth, not the waters, because man ate of the fruit of the earth against the prohibition, and did not drink of the waters, and in the waters the sin contracted from the fruit of the earth was to be washed away. Land animals too have more of the curse than water creatures, because they live more from the cursed earth. Hence Christ after the resurrection ate of fish, not of a land animal.

« Cursed is the earth in thy work. » [n: (GREG., Moral. lib. IV, c. 5, 6.)] God curses the sinning man, etc., *as far as* and the flame burned up the two captains of fifty.

« In labors, » etc. [n: (RAB.)] Thorns were there before, but not so as to bring labor upon man: for him they were, after the sin, [0096A] for labor and affliction, as were serpents and whatever things are harmful, harmless before. And it is to be noted that on Adam an outward penalty is laid: « Cursed is the earth in thy work; » but on the woman an inward one, when it is said: « I will multiply thy sorrows, » etc., because, having been seduced, she seduced her husband.

VERS. 19.-- « Dust thou art, » etc. [n: (GREG., ubi supra.)] Man was so constituted, etc., *as far as* old age passes over into death.

VERS. 20.-- « And Adam called the name of his wife Eve, » etc. [n: (AUG., ubi supra. )] These were the words of the first man, since she is the mother of all the living, as though he were rendering the reason of the name imposed by himself, namely why he had called her Eve.

*Mystically. [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 21.)]* [0096B] After the sin and the judge's sentence Adam called his wife Eve, etc., *as far as* it is expressed in the skins which are stripped from dead bodies.

VERS. 21.-- « He made also, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. lib. XI, c. 39.)] This too was done for the sake of signification, and in deeds it is asked what was done and what it signifies, and in words what was said and what it signifies.

[n: (STRAB.)] They themselves made girdles for themselves, that they might hide their sin: but God made tunics of skin, with which he clothed their whole body, because he justly condemned them both in body and in soul. But it is a foolish question to ask how, or by whose working, the skins were drawn off from the animals: for he who founded all things out of nothing made these [0096C] how and in what manner he willed.

VERS. 22.-- « And he said: Behold Adam is as one of us, » etc. [n: (AUG., ibid., c. 41.)] The plural number is used on account of the Trinity, etc., *as far as* as in the Church men are wont to be removed from the visible sacraments by the Church's discipline.

[n: (AUG., ubi supra, c. 42.)] It is a wonder if Adam was spiritual in mind, etc., *as far as* which thought the false to be true.

« As one of us. » [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., lib. II, c. 21.)] It can be understood in two ways, « as one of us, » that is, God, etc., *as far as* until by God's mercy he should live again who had been dead.

[n: (RUPERT.)] Lest he eat of the tree of life, and, this being done, Adam, made such and so wretched, live for ever. For what [0096D] if, having been made such, he should live for ever? assuredly nothing but eternal evil. For being already made wretched, if he be also eternal, what will he have but eternal wretchedness? Let us spare him, lest in this way it be ill with him « as one of us; » lest, as each of us is eternal (for the Father is eternal, the Son eternal, the Holy Spirit eternal), so he too be eternal, and for this the wicked eye of the devil laugh that he is truthful, who promised him the likeness of God. For it is indeed an evil for him to be temporal, but it would be worst of all to be eternal. Exceedingly evil and false is this likeness of God. Far better is it for him to be wholly unlike God. How? Namely thus, that, because he is wretched, he be also temporal, this is to be wholly unlike God. [0097A] For God is both eternal and happy, and his is an eternal happiness, a happy eternity. Of these the devil lost the one, that is, happiness, but eternity he did not lose; and his is an eternal unhappiness, an unhappy eternity. Let us spare the man, I say; and because he has lost happiness, let us cut off eternity also from the unhappy one, that in neither may he be « as one of us; » ours is an eternal happiness, a happy eternity; let his be a temporal misery, or a miserable temporality; that eternity may then be restored to him to his profit, when happiness has been recovered. In great wrath, therefore, the Lord remembered great mercy, in this very thing, that he did not grant the tree of life to wretched man: him indeed whom he willed to be so unlike himself that he should not be in both respects like the devil, that is, that man should not live, equally with the devil, [0097B] until the last judgment, and, equally with him, pass over without temporal death to eternal burning.

VERS. 23.-- « And he sent him forth, » etc. [n: (AUG., de Gen. contra Manich., l. II, c. 22.)] Into a place suited to him; as very often a bad man, when he has begun to live among the good, if he has been unwilling to be changed for the better, is driven from the congregation of the good by the weight of his own depraved habit.

VERS. 24.-- « And he cast out Adam. » [n: (HIERON. Quaest. Hebr., tom. III.)] LXX: « And he cast him out, and made him dwell over against the paradise of pleasure, and he set the cherubim, and the flaming sword, which is turned about to guard the way of the tree of life. » There is another sense in the Hebrew; for it says, « and the Lord cast out, » namely Adam, « and [0097C] made the cherubim and the flaming sword to dwell before the paradise of pleasure. » Not that he made Adam dwell over against the paradise of pleasure, but that, when Adam had been cast out, he placed the cherubim and the flaming sword before the doors of paradise, that no one might be able to enter.

« And he placed, » etc. [n: (AUG., ut supra. )] This is to be believed to have been done through the heavenly powers even in the visible paradise, so that through angelic ministry there was there a certain fiery guard, yet not in vain, but because it signifies something of the spiritual paradise.

« He placed, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] That is, through the ministry of angels he established a fiery guard. The flaming sword is the tribulations, griefs and labors of this life, by which we are exercised; or the sentence of just condemnation which was given to man, who was so condemned [0097D] that he could be redeemed by Christ's passion. Whence the sword is called turning, because it can be removed. For it was removed for Enoch and Elias, and daily it is removed for the faithful who pass from this life to the blessedness above. Nor does he say a flame and a sword, but « a flaming sword: » for they who return to paradise must burn away with the fire of the Holy Spirit all the concupiscences of this life, and cut off with the sword of the word of God all things harmful.

« And he placed, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen., tom. V.)] The cherubim, fullness of knowledge, this is charity, which is the fullness of the divine law. The flaming sword, temporal punishments, which are turning, since times are revolving. To the tree of life, therefore, there is no return [0098A] except through the cherubim, that is, the fullness of knowledge, that is, charity: and through the turning sword, that is, temporal endurance.



## CHAPTER IV.

VERS. 1.-- « But Adam, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. IX de Gen. ad litt., c. 4)]. In paradise virginity, outside it marriage, etc., *as far as* but afterwards begotten in exile.

« She conceived and bore, » etc. Cain and Abel, begotten of one mother, are a figure of all men who are propagated into this life from the root of sin; and some are to love the earthly city and its death-bearing delights, and, so far as in them lies, to possess them by ambition; these Cain signifies, who is interpreted *possession.* Others are to seek the city to come, and, mourning over the miseries of this habitation, [0098B] to pass over with all their desire to the glory to come — these Abel signifies, who is interpreted *mourning,* and is the later: because that which is spiritual is not first, but that which is animal. Hence Cain is called « a husbandman, » that is, one bent upon earthly works; but Abel « a shepherd of sheep, » that is, one loving simplicity and innocence.

*Allegorically.* [n: (ISID., in Gen. )] The two sons of Adam express two peoples, namely the Jewish, the elder by birth, etc., *as far as* as though reason did not permit the depraved motion to come to effect, but strove to subdue it.

VERS. 2.-- « And Abel was, » etc. In whom are signified those who mourn over their earthly dwelling. For Abel is interpreted *mourning,* or *vapor,* because he was a mourning to his parents, or, like a vapor, quickly vanished away.

[0098C]VERS. 3, 4.-- « And it came to pass, » etc. « Abel also offered, » etc. Because the just man in all that he does strives to please God through faith and charity (from which the other virtues arise, and without which they profit nothing), which is signified in the fat that was offered; whence psal. LXII: « As with marrow and fatness let my soul be filled. » Without charity the soul is lean and languid, and in nothing will it please God.

« And the Lord had respect to Abel, » etc. [n: (HIER., Quaest. Hebr. in Gen., tom. III.)] Whence could Cain have known this, unless Theodotion's rendering were true: « And the Lord set fire upon Abel and upon his sacrifice, upon Cain and his sacrifice he did not set fire? » Now we read that fire was wont to come from heaven to devour the sacrifice, as at the dedication of the temple [0098D] under Solomon, and when Elias built an altar on Mount Carmel.

[n: (ISID., ut supra. )] If thou offer rightly, and do not rightly divide, thou hast sinned: because, even though the Jews formerly offered those things rightly, in this they are guilty, that they did not distinguish the New Testament from the Old.

VERS. 5, 6, 7.-- . . . . « In the doors. » [n: (ALC.)] That is, sin will accompany thee going in and going out, nor will the Lord keep thy coming in and thy going out.

VERS. 8.-- « And Cain said, » etc. [n: (ISID., Quaest. in Gen., tom. V.)] He does not acquiesce in the Lord when he says, Be still, etc., *as far as* Christ therefore, the head of the younger people, is slain by the elder-born.

VERS. 9.-- « And the Lord said to Cain. » *(ISID., ubi [0099A] supra.)* The Lord questions Cain, not as one ignorant that he may learn, but as a judge that he may punish. To this day the Jews, when we question them out of the Scriptures concerning Christ, answer that they do not know what we say. Cain's lying ignorance is the Jews' false denial: but they would be Christ's keepers, if they believed with the heart and confessed with the mouth.

VERS. 10.-- « The voice of the blood, » etc. Great is the power of justice, which demands vengeance for the blood of the innocent.

VERS. 11.-- « Now therefore thou shalt be cursed, » etc. [n: (RAB.)] It is to be noted that in Adam's sin the earth is cursed. But Cain is cursed, because, knowing the condemnation of the first transgression, he added fratricide.

VERS. 11.-- « Now therefore thou shalt be cursed, » etc. By the place are signified the things which are in the place; as though thou shalt be cursed [0099B] by those who are upon the earth, who opened their mouth in the confession of sins, to receive the blood which was shed for the remission of them from the hand of the persecutor, who would not be under grace, but under the law, so that he should be cursed by the Church: which understands and shows him to be cursed: for they who are of the works of the law are under a curse.

VERS. 12.-- « When thou hast tilled, » etc. The reference is made to the word, not to the sense, because "earth" is taken here in one way, above in another: there for the Church, here for the earthly working which the Jew still practises; that is, carnal circumcision, the passover, the sabbath, and the like, which have a hidden power of understanding the grace of God, but do not give fruit to the Jews, who are unwilling to pass over to Christ. By "earth" the flesh of Christ can be understood, in [0099C] which the Jews by crucifying wrought our salvation. « For he died for our sins, and rose again for our justification: » [var: Vulg. Rom 4:25 *Qui traditus est*, "who was delivered up"] and because they denied him, they were not justified.

« A vagabond and a fugitive, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] Scattered among the nations, a fugitive from Jerusalem. LXX: « groaning and trembling. » Groaning, from the grief of the kingdom he has lost; trembling, at the multitude of the Christian people, lest thou be slain. « My iniquity is greater, » etc. He exaggerates his fault, but confesses it proudly, and does not beg pardon.

VERS. 13.-- « My iniquity is greater. » [n: (ISID.)] Adding sins to sins he despairs, and does not believe that he can obtain pardon, which is blasphemy against the Holy Spirit, which is not forgiven in this world nor in the world to come: because he thinks that God either will not forgive, [0099D] or cannot, as though either he could not do all things, or begrudged salvation. He does not count it a grievous thing that God is angry with him, but fears that he may be found and killed.

VERS. 14.-- « Every one therefore that shall find me, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Because from the trembling of the body and the agitation of a frenzied mind, whosoever shall find me will know me to be guilty of death.

VERS. 15.-- « It shall not be so. » That is, thou shalt not die quickly, as thou wishest, for a remedy of thy torment, but he shall be punished impersonally: that is, punishment shall be made of thee sevenfold, because thou shalt live in torment until the seventh generation, in which thou shalt be slain by Lamech; therefore every one who shall kill — supply the word — shall free thee from that torment of an evil conscience. This sense [0100A] appears more evidently. According to the LXX. « Every one that killeth Cain shall pay seven vengeances, » that is, whosoever shall kill thee in the seventh generation shall release thee from torment by death, thou who art to be reserved in life until that time, only for punishment, which punishment would be quickly ended if thou wert quickly slain. So he who should punish the Jews with visible death would take away from them the seven vengeances by which they are bound because of the guilt of Christ slain, because he would lighten the miseries and dispersions which they suffer.

« Every one that killeth Cain, » etc. LXX: « Shall pay seven vengeances. » Aquila, « sevenfold; » Symmachus, « sevenfold; » Theodotion, « by a week » — so they render it, wherein it is signified that he shall be punished long and completely, lest any one dare [0100B] to repeat a homicide.

« He that killeth Cain, » etc. [n: (HIERON., epist. ad Damas. )] If any one kill thee « he shall be punished sevenfold, » that is, he shall be punished with the heaviest vengeance: a man ought not to have killed him for whom God reserved life, either for his own punishment, or for the correction of others.

« But every one that killeth Cain. » [n: (ISID., ubi supra. )] It is to be read by ellipsis, etc., *as far as* and that is fulfilled: « Scatter them in thy strength [n: (Psal. LIII)]. »

[n: (HIER., ubi supra. )] That Aquila put « sevenfold, » etc., *as far as* and might deserve to be absolved.

« And the Lord set a mark upon Cain, » etc. [n: (ISID., Quaest. in Gen., tom. V.)] Though all the nations subdued by the Romans passed over into their rites, the Jewish nation, whether under Gentiles or under Christian kings, [0100C] did not leave the sign of circumcision and of carnal observance, etc., *as far as* where paradise was planted.

« He dwelt in the land at the eastern quarter of Eden. » [n: (HIER., ubi supra. )] LXX: « He dwelt in the land Ναΐδ Naid, in the Hebrew it is called ( *nod* ), that is, unstable and wavering, and of uncertain seat. It is not the land Naid, as our common folk think, but the Lord's sentence is fulfilled, while he strays to and fro a fugitive.

VERS. 17.-- « And Cain knew his wife. » [n: (ISID., ibid.)] Figuratively the offspring of the impious, seeking a foundation in the very origin of the world, built in this life a city which he called Henoch, which is interpreted *dedication,* because in their first beginnings they dedicate, while in this life which is before them they plant the root of the heart, [0100D] that here they may flourish to their wish, and wither away from the glory to come. But Abraham dwelt in little cottages, because the saints know themselves to be guests and pilgrims, awaiting the city that has a foundation, whose builder is God.

« And he built a city, » etc. [n: (AUG., lib. XV de Civitate, cap. 8, tom. VII.)] It is asked with what helpers he built it, if there were not more men than Scripture says. But it is to be known that there were then far more, because by living long they were able to beget more. Nor is it a wonder, since the children of Israel are read to have been so multiplied in four hundred years that the army could not be numbered.

VERS. 18.-- « Moreover Enoch, » etc. [n: (HIERON.)] Not [0101A] from all these are sure mysteries carved out, nor would they be found, unless first certain roots of the history were laid down; nor wouldst thou gather fruit from the branches of the trees, unless thou hadst first planted the trunks.

VERS. 19.-- « Who took two wives, » etc. [n: (ISID., ut supra. )] It is to be noted that the generations from Adam through Cain are terminated in the eleventh, in which transgression is signified. For Lamech is the seventh, to whom three sons and one daughter are added, that the eleventh may be filled out, by which sin is signified: for the generation is closed also by a woman, who was the beginning of sin, through which is the pleasure of the flesh, which resists the spirit. Whence she is called Noema, that is, *pleasure.* In this offspring Lamech first, against custom, against nature, committed adultery through bigamy.

[0101B]VERS. 20.-- « And Ada bore Jabel, » etc. [n: (HIER. in quaest. Hebr. )] Of bigamy is born Jabel dwelling in tents, which, being changeable, figure the instability of those who are carried about with every wind of doctrine. There is born also Tubal, who was the inventor of music, etc., *as far as* like Jabel, Tubal and Tubalcain.

VERS. 21.-- « And his brother's name was Jubal. » [n: (RAB.)] Josephus writes that he wrote music on two columns, the one of stone, the other of brick, of which the one would not be dissolved by the flood, the other which would not be dissolved by fire: which two judgments Adam had foretold were to come.

VERS. 22.-- . . . « Tubalcain. [n: (RAB.)] Who according to Josephus practised the arts of war, and fittingly taught the smith's craft; he also invented certain things which [0101C] belong to the concupiscence of the eyes.



## CHAPTER IV. (cont.)

VERS. 23.-- « And Lamech said, » etc. [n: (RAB.)] The Hebrews say that Lamech by long living fell into a darkening of the eyes, and had a young man as his leader and the director of his way. Practising the hunt, therefore, he aimed an arrow where the young man pointed, and by chance killed Cain lurking among the bushes; and this is what he says: « I have slain a man to my own wounding, » that is, by the wound which I inflicted I killed not a beast but a man; whence also, kindled with fury, he killed the young man.

VERS. 23.-- . . . « I have slain a man, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] That is, Cain: « to my own wounding, » because for him I shall be wounded, that is, slain; « and a young man to my own bruising, » because for him too I shall be condemned.

VERS. 24.-- « Sevenfold vengeance shall be given, » etc. [0101D] [n: (ALC.)] *Allegorically.* The sin of Lamech (who is interpreted *humbled* ) signifies the casting down of the human race, which fell when Adam transgressed, and could not rise, neither by the law, or by circumcision, nor by its own justice, until, after seventy-seven generations according to Luke, Christ, coming into the world, blotted out sin by the shedding of his blood; whence Matth. XVIII:

« I say not to thee seven times, but until seventy times seven times, » that is, that the Jew is to be received after seventy-seven vengeances, if he shall come to himself again and seek Christ's indulgence. The number seven signifies universality: by which, the eleventh being multiplied, there comes the seventy-seventh, in which transgression of every kind is signified; [0102A] which nevertheless in the Gospel is commanded to be forgiven to one who repents.

[n: (HIERON., epist. ad Damas. )] They say that from Adam to Christ there are seventy-seven generations according to Luke. As therefore in the seventh generation Cain's sin was loosed: « For God will not judge twice for the same thing, » and he who has once received in his life will not suffer in death the same torments which he suffered in his life: so also Lamech, that is, the sin of the whole world, shall be loosed by Christ's coming. A certain Hebrew told me that seventy-seven souls came forth from Lamech, who all perished in the flood.

VERS. 25.-- « He knew also again, » etc. Through Seth, who is interpreted *resurrection,* is signified the life of Christ from the dead: which is signified in the mother's words: [0102B] « The Lord hath given me another seed, » [var: Vulg. Gn 4:25 reads *Deus*, "God"] as though the Church said: I who bore mourning for the dead rejoice over him raised again.

VERS. 26.-- « Whom he called Enos, » etc. Enos is interpreted *man:* who « began to call upon the name of the Lord, » because through the death of Christ, which Abel signified, and through the resurrection, which Seth signifies, man becomes one confessing the Lord as founder, and as restorer of the life that was lost.

[n: (HIERON.)] « Whom he called Enos. » Enos is interpreted *man,* who « began to call upon the name of the Lord: » as Adam is interpreted *man,* so also Enos according to the Hebrew truth is *man,* or *husband;* and beautifully, because he had this name, it is said that then was the beginning of calling upon the name of the Lord: although very many of the Hebrews [0102C] think that then for the first time idols were fashioned in the name of the Lord and in his likeness.

## CHAPTER V.

VERS. 1, 2.-- « This is the book of the generation of Adam. » He was made to the likeness of God. Others, to the likeness of Adam. It is as it were a conclusion from what has gone before, with a recapitulation. « In the day that God created man. » For in the day he was created, because he was set in the brightness of divine contemplation. « To the likeness of God. » Because he gave him reason, by which, discerning good and evil, he might love the things understood, and not hand over the things loved to forgetfulness, and in this retain the likeness of God. But after by disobedience [0102D] he had blotted out this likeness, being made earthly he begot earthly children. Whence, such as the earthly man is, such also are the earthly.

[n: (CHRYSOST., hom. 21 in Gen., tom. IV.)] Consider how he used the same words which he used also at the beginning, that he might teach us that he does not deign to remember those generations after this, as having been made reprobate; but from Seth, who has now been born to them, he begins the genealogy, that from this thou mayest learn how great account God takes of the human race, and how he turns away from those whose mind is bloody.

VERS. 3-23.-- « And Adam lived, » etc. [n: (HIERON., in Quaest. Hebr. )]. It is to be known that up to the flood, where in our codices someone is said to have begotten at « two hundred » and the years that run beyond, in the Hebrew [0103A] it has « a hundred years » and the rest which follow.

« And he begot, » etc. As though he too bore the image of the earthly man, he who was put in the place of the just Abel, and by the interpretation of his name signifies *Christ.* But all bear the image of the earthly man before that of the heavenly, although many before the coming of Christ had through faith the same grace by which Christians are saved.

[n: (STRAB.)] They report that after Abel's death Adam vowed that he would not know his wife further nor beget a son. But at God's bidding he broke the vow, that the Son of God might be born from him, and man be redeemed; for he ought not to be born of the stock of Cain. Whence before Seth he is not read to have begotten any one, but after him it is said; because « he begot sons and daughters. » This reckoning of the genealogies [0103B] spreads as it were the branches of the histories, from which in their own places the fruits of the mysteries are gathered. And it is to be noted that in the generation of Seth no woman is set down by name, as in the generation of Cain: for it belongs to the just to do all things manfully, to usurp nothing womanish, nothing frail. This generation also proceeds by the tenth up to Noe, in whom the fulfilling of the commandments is signified, as by the eleventh in the generation of Cain transgression is signified. In the generation of the unjust Enoch is set first, who is interpreted *dedication,* because the reprobate fix the root of their hope in the present life, and wither away from the love of the unfading inheritance. Of whom it is said, John V: « He saw the fool with a firm root, and cursed his beauty forthwith. » [var: Vulg. Job 5:3, where the words stand in the first person — *Ego vidi … et maledixi*, "I have seen … and I cursed"] In the generation of the just Enoch is set seventh from Adam, because these [0103C] do not seek a city that abides here, but stretch out their expectation to the end of time, which is terminated by a sevenfold revolution, when the eighth eternity shall have succeeded. Whence this Enoch, who is set seventh, and was translated because he pleased God, signifies the seventh rest, to which after this life he who perseveringly cleaves to God is transferred without delay. The interpretation also of the names of this generation signifies the same: Adam is interpreted *man* or *red earth;* Seth, *placing* or *resurrection;* Enos, *man;* Cainan, *lamentation;* Malalehel, *praising God;* Jared, *strengthened;* Enoch, *dedication;* Mathusalam, *sending forth of death;* Lamech, *humbled;* Noe, *rest.* Man therefore, proceeding from the earth, rising again from sins, will be a man calling upon God and weeping for his former sins: and so [0103D] praising God, strengthened by the Spirit of God, he is dedicated unto life eternal. And, a conqueror of death, made subject to God, he possesses everlasting rest, which Noe signifies, who is interpreted *rest,* and is numbered in the tenth place, because through the fulfilling of the commandments the kingdom of heaven is possessed.

VERS. 24.-- « And he walked with God, » etc. [n: (ISID., in Gen.)] Enoch, the seventh from Adam, pleasing God, etc., *as far as* for three times four make it.

[n: (ALCUIN.)] Enoch is preserved from death for so long a time, in which it is shown that man would always have lived if he had not sinned.

VERS. 25-30.-- « Mathusala also lived, » etc. [n: (AUG.)] It is asked how Mathusala, according to the [0104A] reckoning of the years, could have lived after the flood, since all perished except those who entered into the ark. But this question the faultiness of the codices has bred: for not only in the Hebrew is it found otherwise, but also in the LXX rendering, in a few codices Mathusala is found to have died seven years before the flood. That according to the LXX the years of Mathusala are reckoned beyond the flood, comes of this, that he signifies Christ, whose life feels no age, so that in the greater ones too he might seem not to have felt the flood.

VERS. 31.-- « And all the days were, » etc. [n: (HIERON., in Quaest. Hebr., tom. III.)] « And all the days of Mathusala which he lived were nine hundred sixty-nine years, and he died. » According to a careful computation he is reported to have lived [0104B] fourteen years after the flood, etc., *as far as* in that year, namely, in which the flood began to be.

« But Noe, when he was five hundred years old, » etc. [n: (Ibid.)]

Noe is interpreted *rest,* because under him all the works behind were at rest through the flood.



## CHAPTER VI.

VERS. 1.-- « And when men had begun to be multiplied upon the earth. » [n: (HIER., in Quaest. Hebr. )] The Hebrew word ( *Elohim* ) is of both numbers: for it signifies *God* and *gods:* therefore Aquila dared to say « sons of gods, » understanding holy gods or angels, whom Symmachus follows, saying: « The sons of the mighty seeing the daughters of men. »

[0104C]VERS. 2.-- « The sons of God seeing, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] By the sons of God are understood the religious sons of Seth, who, overcome by concupiscence, took wives from the daughters of men, that is, from the stock of Cain, and begot mighty men, immense, namely, in body, proud in strength, disordered in manners, who are called giants. It is not incredible that men of this kind were procreated by men, not by angels or by certain demons who are shameless toward women, since after the flood too the bodies not only of men but also of women existed of incredible size.

VERS. 3, 4.-- « And God said: My spirit shall not remain, » etc. [n: (HIER., ibid.)] Hebr. ( *loiadon* ). « My spirit shall not judge men for ever, since they are flesh. » That is, because [0104D] the condition of men is frail, etc., *as far as* but of those whom he was about to destroy by the flood.

« And his days shall be, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] Before the flood, namely, for the doing of penance. But because they persevered in malice before the appointed term, they were destroyed in the hundredth year.

VERS. 5.-- « And the Lord seeing, » etc. [var: Vulg. Gn 6:5 reads *Deus*, "God"] Repentance does not fall upon God, nor grief of heart, whose sentence concerning all things is as fixed as his foreknowledge is certain: but Scripture uses accustomed words, fitting itself to our littleness, that from things known we may come to know things unknown. For God's anger is not a perturbation of mind, but the judgment which is inflicted upon the sinner: and the thought of his heart is the unchangeable reason of things that are to be changed.

[0105A]VERS. 6-8.-- « It repented him that, » etc. [n: (GREG., Moral. lib. V)]. Very often in sacred discourse the wise draw the counsel of God from the wise men of the world. So now, for man's profit, the very founder of men takes upon himself the voice of human passion, though it is certain that he who beholds all things before they have come to pass has done nothing of which he should repent and change his mind. As therefore it is no wonder if spiritual men very often use the words of carnal men, so neither is it to be wondered at if the creator himself of all things, that he may draw the flesh to his own understanding, keeps or takes up in himself the speech of the flesh.

VERS. 9-12.-- « Noe a just man. » Here by his acts he signifies Christ, who says Matth. XI: « Learn of me, because I am meek and humble of heart. » He alone is found just, to whom because of his justice seven men [0105B] are given. Christ too is just and perfect, to whom seven Churches, enlightened by the sevenfold Spirit, are granted together into one Church. Noe is delivered through water and wood, and his household with him; so the household of Christ through baptism and the cross.

« In his generations. » As though not of consummate justice, but according to his own generation.

VERS. 13.-- « I will destroy them with, » etc. The doctors hand down that the greenness and fruitfulness of the earth was far inferior after the flood; and that therefore the eating of flesh was granted to men, whereas before they lived only on the fruits of the earth.

VERS. 14.-- « Make thee an ark of timber. » Namely of timber strong and indissoluble and well compacted; which another translation calls « square, » so that it should be dissolved neither by the force of the winds [0105C] nor by the inundation.

« Little rooms, » etc. [n: (STRAB.)] LXX: « Thou shalt make in the ark things of two chambers and of three chambers: of two chambers in the lower parts, of three chambers in the upper. » They were therefore divided into five dwellings: the first, that is the lowest, was the dung-place, into which the dung flowed down, lest those who were in the ark should be harmed by the stench; the second, the storeroom; the third, of the fierce animals and of serpents; the fourth, of the tame; the fifth, that is the highest, of men and of birds. The door, where the two-chambered and the three-chambered parts were joined, that is, between the storeroom and the dwelling of the beasts. [n: (AUG.)] In the first dwelling, in the lower parts, the ark was of one chamber, in the second, above the lower dwelling, of two chambers. But in the third, above the second, of three chambers. Others say that in the lower parts [0105D] there were two little rooms, and that so the ark was of two chambers. Of these the lower received the dung, the second the food, which was called the storeroom; in the upper parts it was of three chambers, where men and animals were contained with a fitting distinction.

[n: (ISID. in Gen., tom. V.)] Noe built the ark of imperishable timbers, etc., *as far as* prophesied in the fifth, evangelized in the sixth.

VERS. 15. « Of three hundred cubits, » etc. [n: (ISID., ibid.)] In the three hundred cubits the wood of the cross can be shown, etc., *as far as* because there is one God from whom are all things, and one faith, and one baptism.

[n: (GREG. hom. XVI in Ezech.)] The ark which was commanded to be made three hundred cubits in length, fifty [0106A] in breadth, thirty in height, etc., *as far as* and through whom all advance who have known themselves to be sinners.

VERS. 16-18.-- « In a cubit thou shalt finish the top of it. » So the Church, gathered into unity, is lifted up and made perfect; whence Matth. XI: « He that gathereth not with me, scattereth. »

The door of the ark, » etc. For no one enters into the Church except through the sacrament of the remission of sins, which flowed forth from the opened side.

« Upper chambers and third stories, » etc. LXX: « Things of two chambers and of three chambers. » For out of all nations the Church gathers a multitude either two-parted, from the circumcision and the uncircumcision; or three-parted, from the three sons of Noe, by whose offspring the world is filled. But the lower parts [0106B] of the ark are divided, because in this earthly life there is a diversity of nations: but at the top all are consummated into one, because Christ is all things and in all.

[n: (ALCUIN.)] Fittingly the ark was brought to a point at the top, as with a certain narrow roof, that it might scatter the threats of the rains, and, standing below in the waters four-square with stability, could be neither tilted nor sunk by the driving of the winds, nor by the onset of the waves, nor by the restlessness of the animals that were within.

[n: (GREG. in Moral.)] The ark broad in the lower parts, narrow in the upper, etc., *as far as* great heaps of chaff, which are burned up with fire.

[n: (GREG.)] But morally, he who turns himself away from the love of the world builds in his heart the ark of salvation, having in [0106C] himself length, that is, the faith of the Trinity, and length of life and of immortality; breadth in charity, by which he can do good even to enemies; height in hope, by which he may raise himself to heavenly things and refer the sum of his acts to the One.

VERS. 19-20.-- « And of all, » etc. [n: (AUG. l. XV de Civit. Dei, c. ult., t. VII.)] It is asked concerning the tiniest creatures, so in the Church's sacraments the good and the bad are engaged.

VERS. 21, 22.-- « Take thou therefore, » etc. It is wont to be asked concerning the animals which are thought to feed only on flesh, whether they were there beyond the number without transgression of the commandment, which the necessity of feeding the others had compelled to be shut in; or, what is rather to be believed, whether besides flesh there could have been foods which would suit [0106D] all. For many animals, whose food is flesh, feed on fruits and apples, especially on the fig and on chestnuts. A just and wise man, therefore, divinely admonished, was able to find out and lay up the nourishment which suited each, besides flesh. And hunger compels all to feed, and there is nothing which God cannot make sweet and wholesome, who would even grant by divine power that they should live without food.



## CHAPTER VII.

VERS. 1-3.-- « And the Lord said, » etc. « Of all clean living creatures thou shalt take seven and seven, » etc. [n: (RAB.)] Not a doubled seven, that is fourteen, but only seven ought we to [0107A] understand. Likewise two and two, not four, but only two, the male and the female. And it is to be noted that the clean animals are brought in by an odd number, that is by the number seven, but the unclean by an even one; because an odd number portends the appearance of virtue, but an even one weakness. The clean are the more numerous, that Noah, on going out of the ark, might have what he could offer in sacrifice and eat.

[n: (ISID. in Gen.)] « Of all living creatures, » etc. Not that the good are more numerous than the bad, etc., *as far as* which day is, reckoned from the passion, the third among the days that roll round through all time, and the first, and the eighth.

[n: (AUG. in Gen.)] It is asked: could so great an ark be built in a hundred years by four men, that is, by Noah and his three sons? But if it could not, it was no great matter to employ other workmen, although, [0107B] once the wage of their work had been got, they cared not whether Noah was acting wisely or to no purpose, and did not enter into it, because they did not believe what he believed.

VERS. 4.-- « I will rain, » etc. [n: (ISID. Quaest. in Gen., c. 7.)] Because every guilt against the decalogue is committed throughout the whole world, which is divided into four parts: and four times ten make forty; whether the guilt be contracted out of prosperity, which pertains to the days; or out of adversity, which pertains to the nights, it is washed away in baptism.

VERS. 5-6.-- « Noah therefore did all things, » etc. « And he was six hundred years old, » etc. [n: (ISID., Ibid)]. Noah was five hundred years old when the Lord commanded him to make the ark, etc., *as far as* which is the square of three.

[0107C]VERS. 7-9.-- « Noah went in, and his sons, his wife, » etc. [n: (RAB.)] It is to be noted that Noah goes into the ark apart with his sons, and their wives apart. Therefore it is said distinctly: « Noah went in, and his sons, » and it is distinctly added, « his wife and the wives of his sons. » But they went out mingled, the men with the women, for so it is written: « Noah went out, and his wife.[var: Vulg. Gn 8:18 reads *Egressus est ergo Noe, et filii ejus : uxor illius, et uxores filiorum ejus cum eo*, "Noah therefore went out, and his sons: his wife, and the wives of his sons with him."] For there is a time of embracing and a time of abstaining[var: Vulg. Ecl 3:5 reads *tempus amplexandi, et tempus longe fieri ab amplexibus*, "a time to embrace, and a time to be far from embraces."] [n: (Eccli. III)]. »

VERS. 10.-- « And when they had passed, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] Because we are baptized in the hope of the rest to come, which the seventh day signifies, all flesh outside the ark was consumed by the flood; because outside the Church the water of baptism, though it be the same water, avails not unto salvation, but unto ruin.

[0107D]VERS. 11-14.-- « In the six hundredth year, » etc. *Allegorically.* It is to be noted that in the six hundredth year the waters of the flood overflowed, because in the sixth age of the world the sacrament of baptism was first entrusted to us, and this on the seventeenth day of the second month, because the number ten signifies the decalogue. The number seven, on account of the Sabbath, signifies the seventh rest, and because in the decalogue the renunciation of vices is seen, whosoever is initiated in baptism professes, for the sake of eternal rest, to renounce all vices.

« The cataracts. » Windows, that is to say clouds, which were opened, that from them unwonted and greater rains might be poured out. But cataracts are properly the mouths of the Nile, though by misuse they are set for all windows.

[0108A] [n: (RAB.)] Concerning the ark it is commonly asked: with so great a capacity as is described, could it carry all the animals that are said to have entered, and their food? Origen resolves this by the geometrical cubit, asserting that it was not said in vain that Moses was learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians, who loved geometry, and that the geometrical cubit contains as much as six of ours.

VERS. 15, 16.-- « Two and two, » etc. [n: (AUG. ubi supra.)] He commanded above that seven and seven of the clean, and two and two of the unclean, be taken. How then does he now say that two and two entered, whether of the clean or of the unclean? But this is referred not to the number of the clean and the unclean, but to the male and the female. For in all there are two, male and female.

[0108B]But it is to be noted that what is said, « in which was the spirit of life, » is said not of men only, but of cattle also: because some would have that which is written, « he breathed into his face the spirit of life, »[var: Vulg. Gn 2:7 reads *inspiravit in faciem ejus spiraculum vitae*, "breathed into his face the breath of life."] understood of the Holy Spirit; but certain codices have better, « the breath of life. »

VERS. 17-19.-- « Forty days, » etc. [n: (ALC.)] The rain overflowed by the number forty, which shows the tribulation of penance. After forty days Noah opened the window, signifying that heaven is opened to them that fast. Whence Moses, Elias, and the Saviour himself consecrated fasts by the number forty, as being necessary in three times: before the law, under the law, under grace.

VERS. 20-22-- « Higher by fifteen cubits, » etc. [0108C] [n: (AUG. in Gen.)] If the earth of Mount Olympus (as history relates) could reach into the space of the tranquil air, etc., *as far as* to the holding fast of rest and of faith in the resurrection.

« Fifteen. » There are those who think that neither the quality of the earth nor the height of the mountains was, before the flood, such and so great as it is today.

VERS. 23, 24.-- « They were destroyed from the earth. » [n: (ALC.)] Therefore the animals of the land perished rather than those of the water, because they live off the accursed earth.



## CHAPTER VIII.

VERS. 1-3.-- « And God remembered « Noah, » etc. « He brought a spirit over the earth, » etc. [n: (ALCUIN., quaest. in Gen.)] It can be understood of that spirit of which it was said, « The Spirit of God was borne over [0108D] the waters: » then it was borne, that, the waters being gathered into their own *[Al., unum]* place, the earth might appear: now it is brought, that, the waters of the flood being taken away, it might lay bare the face of the earth. By the spirit a wind can be understood, according to that of Psalm CVI: « And the spirit of the storm stood, » by whose blasts, namely, the water would draw back.

VERS. 4.-- « And the ark rested in the seventh month, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] It signifies the sevenfold rest, and, because the perfect rest there, the number of the aforesaid squaring is repeated also. For on the twenty-seventh day of the second month the sacrament is committed, and again on the twenty-seventh day of the seventh month it is confirmed, when the ark rested: for what is promised in hope is exhibited in fact.

[0109A] [n: (AUG.)] It is asked whether these things were done after the hundred and fifty days, or whether by recapitulation all those things are recalled which began to be done after the forty days of rain, so that this alone pertains to the « hundred and fifty days », that the water was raised as far as the mountains themselves, either from the fountains of the deep, the rain having now ceased, or because it remained at that height with no wind drying it. But the rest of the things that have been said were not all done after the hundred and fifty days, but recalled: things which began to be done from the end of the forty days.

VERS. 5, 6.-- « For in the tenth month. » It is to be noted that in the tenth month the tops of the mountains appeared, which the waters of the flood had overtopped by fifteen cubits, because the eminence of the saints understood, through the fulfilling of the precepts, the sacraments of eternal rest and the glory [0109B] of the resurrection (which the wisdom of the proud knew not); whence Psalm CXVIII: « By thy commandments I have understood. » And elsewhere, Luke X: « Thou hast hid these things from the wise and prudent, and hast revealed them to little ones. »

VERS. 7.-- « Who went forth, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] That the raven did not return after forty days, either cut off by the waters or enticed by a floating carcase, figures those made most foul by the uncleanness of covetousness: and therefore that those too intent upon the things which are outside in the world are either rebaptized, or are seduced and held fast by those whom baptism outside the ark, that is, outside the Church, has slain.

[n: (AUG.)] It is asked: did the raven die, or could it in some way live? For if there was earth where it might rest, the dove would likewise have found rest. [0109C] Whence it is conjectured that the raven could settle on a carcase, which the dove by nature shuns.

VERS. 8.-- « He sent forth also. » [n: (ISID.)] The dove sent forth, and returned without having found rest, signifies that through the New Testament rest is not promised to the saints in this world: for it was sent forth after forty days, by which number the life that is passed in the world is designated.

[n: (STRAB.)] The ark contains a raven and a dove, and the Church good men and bad. The raven sent out does not return, because the reprobate, clinging to the enticements of the world, perish. The dove returned, bearing a branch of olive with green leaves in her mouth, because the saints, rejoicing in simplicity, bear the gift of peace in their mouth, saying: [n: (Psal. XIX)] « With them that hated peace I was peaceable: [0109D] when I spoke to them, » etc.

VERS. 9.-- « She returned. » [n: (AUG.)] It is a question: how did the dove not find where to settle, when the tops of the mountains had already been laid bare, according to the order of the narrative? This can be resolved by recapitulation, so that things narrated later are understood to have been done earlier, or rather, they had not yet been dried.

VERS. 10.-- « Having waited, » etc. After other seven days, the dove being let go on account of the sevenfold spiritual working, brought back a fruitful shoot of olive, signifying that many baptized outside the Church, if the richness of charity be not wanting, are at a later time brought back, in the mouth of the dove, as in the kiss of peace, to the fellowship of unity.

[0110A]VERS. 11.-- « Bearing a branch. » [n: (ALC.)] The dove bears a branch of olive to Noah after the flood, the most foul bird having been driven out, because the dove of the Holy Spirit, coming to Christ after his baptism, announces peace with the branch of refreshment and of light.

VERS. 12.-- « And he waited nevertheless other seven days, » etc. [n: (ISID. in Gen.)] The dove let go after another seven days, and not returning, signifies the end of the world, when the rest of the saints shall be, not in the sacrament of hope, in which the Church is now joined together, so long as that is drunk which flowed from the side of Christ, but in the perfection of salvation, when the kingdom shall be delivered up to God and the Father, so that in that clear contemplation of truth we may have need of no mysteries.

VERS. 13.-- « In the six hundred and first year, » etc. [0110B] [n: (Ibid.)] Of Noah's life, that is, six hundred years being completed, the roof of the ark is opened, etc., *as far as* one being added, on account of the bond of unity.

VERS. 14.-- « In the second month. » *[n: (STRAB.)] Historically.* That is, in May, Noah completed a year in the ark, and on the very day on which he had entered, he went out. For he entered on the twenty-seventh day of the second month; does it say that he went out on the seventh and twentieth day of the same month. But it must be known that, if eleven be added to the present day, such as the moon is today, such will it be on that same day a year hence. When therefore Noah entered, it was the seventeenth moon, or the seventeenth day of the second month. Therefore after a year, eleven being added, it was the twenty-seventh day and the seventeenth moon.

« In the second month, » etc. *Mystically.* As was said above, [0110C] on the seventeenth day of the six hundredth year the earth was soaked with the waters of the flood, but on the twenty-seventh day of the second month it was dried, when it was commanded that they should go out of the ark and receive a compact, that they should no more perish by the waters of a flood. In which it can be understood that by ten and seven (as above) the state of those beginning can be signified, when vices are renounced; by twenty-seven, the advancement of the perfect in the consummation of the virtues. For if thou add ten to ten and seven, they become twenty-seven. For if to the ten precepts by which sins are renounced thou add the evangelical perfection, by which it is commanded not only not to be angry, but even to love one's enemies, and the like, thou hast come to that age of perfection in which thou needest not even the aforesaid sacraments; and going out [0110D] of the ark, that is, passing over from hope to sight, thou shalt find the rest promised in the sacraments.

VERS. 15-19.-- « Go out of the ark, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] They go out joined who entered separated, because « now the flesh lusteth against the spirit, and the spirit against the flesh. »[var: Vulg. Gal 5:17 reads *Caro enim concupiscit adversus spiritum, spiritus autem adversus carnem*, with *enim* and *autem* for Migne's *nunc* and *et*.] But in the resurrection of the just the body will cleave to the spirit in perfect peace, with no want of mutability or concupiscence resisting.

VERS. 20.-- « And taking, » etc. [n: (AUG.)] Clean and unclean animals entered into the ark, but after the going out none are offered to God but the clean. But in that he says: « I will not strike every living soul as I have done, »[var: Vulg. Gn 8:21 reads *non igitur ultra percutiam*, "I will therefore no more strike"; Migne's lemma lacks *ultra*.] and added those things which his goodness grants of its bounty to the unworthy, perhaps the indulgence of the New Testament [0111A] is figured, so that the vengeance that is past belongs to the severity of the law, and this to the goodness of grace.

VERS. 21, 22.-- « For the sense, » etc. [n: (GREG., Moral. libr. XXXVIII.)] Let no one ascribe it to himself if he has overcome his thoughts: because the evil of corruption, which each one has taken up from the rising of carnal desires, he plies as his age advances; and unless the hand of the divine strength repress it the sooner, guilt devours every good of nature into the depth: « Neither he that planteth is anything, nor he that watereth, but God that giveth the increase [n: (I Cor. III)]. »



## CHAPTER IX.

VERS. 1.-- « And God blessed Noah and his sons; » etc. « Increase and multiply. » Man is set over [0111B] the rest of the animals for their terror, as a solace for the vengeance that is past, and lest a few men be overwhelmed by the more numerous beasts, and that they may know that they have dominion over the irrational, but not over the rational.

VERS. 2, 3.-- « And your terror, » etc. [n: (GREG. libr. II Moral. c. 11.)] In powerful men the virtue of humility is great, etc., *as far as* by which they seek not their own glory, but the profit of their subjects.

VERS. 4-8.-- « Flesh with blood. » [n: (ISID.)] All animals are given them for food, because in the calling of the Gentiles to the faith there is no difference, which is figured in the dish of Peter. They are forbidden to eat blood, lest the former life be held as it were stifled within the conscience, but let it be poured out through confession. The eating of flesh seems to have been granted after the flood, on account of [0111C] the barrenness of the earth and the frailty of man.

VERS. 9-12.-- « Behold, I will establish my compact, » etc. God set a testament between himself and men and every living soul, that he might not destroy it by a flood, the bow, namely, which appears in the clouds, which never shines save from the sun. For those perish not in the flood, separated from the Church, who in the prophets and in all the holy Scriptures, as in the clouds of God, acknowledge the glory of Christ and seek not their own. But lest the worshippers of this sun swell up, let them know that Christ is signified by the sun just as by the lion, by the lamb, by the stone, for the sake of likeness, not by any substance of property.

VERS. 13-17.-- « I will set my bow, » etc. [n: (ALC. in Gen.)] God gave the bow for a sign of security, [0111D] lest fearful men should dread to be destroyed by another flood, often beholding the overflowing of the rains. This is set in the heaven, that it may be seen by all, and that in whatever tribulation we may lift up to him the eyes of our heart who dwells in the heavens. But because the bow is not save from the rays of the sun and a moist cloud, it seems that before the flood there had been no rains. The earth, however, could be made fruitful from dew and from the overflowing of the springs.

[n: (STRAB.)] The bow has two colours, blue and fiery, which express two judgments: one of water, which is past; the other of fire, which is believed to be to come at the end of the world: whence the blue colour is on the outside, but the fiery within.

[0112A]VERS. 18-20.-- « These therefore were the sons of Noah, » etc. [n: (HIER. in Q. Hebr.)] Often the Seventy, not being able to turn the letter *heth* into Greek, because it sounds a double aspiration, added the Greek χ *chi*, teaching that in words of this kind an aspiration is to be made. Whence they rendered *Cham* for that which is *Ham,* from whom Egypt is to this day called Ham in the language of the Egyptians.

VERS. 21.-- « And drinking wine, » etc. Christ also was made drunk, when he suffered; laid bare, when he was crucified. « In his own tabernacle, » that is, among those of his own household by blood, then was the mortality of his flesh laid bare. « To the Jews a scandal, to the Gentiles foolishness, but to them that are called, both Jews and Gentiles, » as it were Sem and Japheth, « the power of God and the wisdom of God: because that which is foolish of God is wiser than men. »[var: Vulg. 1Cor 1:23–24 reads *Judaeis quidem scandalum, gentibus autem stultitiam, ipsis autem vocatis Judaeis, atque Graecis Christum Dei virtutem*: *Graecis*, "Greeks," for Migne's second *gentibus*, and *Christum Dei virtutem* in the accusative for his *Dei virtus*.]

[0112B] [n: (ALCUIN.)] After the flood Noah was made drunk, because perhaps he did not know that one could be made drunk with wine. For neither are men read to have used wine before the flood. Therefore it is said of Noah especially that he planted a vineyard. The uncovering of the thigh follows drunkenness, as lust follows satiety.

VERS. 22.-- « And when he had seen, » etc. [n: (PROCOP. in Gen.)] From these circumstances the sin of Cham is enlarged. He holds his parent in contempt, he blabs out how basely his father had lain, nor does he tell it to one of his brothers, but to both: if more had been at hand, he would have uncovered the deed to all: the deed seemed a thing of laughter to him to whom it least ought; that deed was worthy of shame and of caution, lest the infamy of it should spread abroad to the rest: nor does he announce it to those who were of the household, but to outsiders.

[0112C] [n: (ISID.)] The Jews, seeing the death of Christ, mocked. But Sem and Japheth, two peoples believing out of the uncircumcision and out of the circumcision, turned away and covered him, etc., *as far as* « as it were running together into the peace of the corner. »

VERS. 23.-- « But Sem indeed, » etc. [n: (GREG. in Moral.)] We are said to turn away from that which we reprove. What then is it that the sons, with cloaks thrown over their backs, covered their father's shameful parts, coming turned away, unless that to good subjects the evils of their superiors so displease that they hide them, once known, from others: they bear the covering turned away, because, judging the deed and revering their master, they will not see what they cover.

VERS. 24, 25.-- « Cursed be Chanaan the boy, »[var: Vulg. Gn 9:25 reads *Maledictus Chanaan, servus servorum erit fratribus suis*, with no word answering to *puer*.] etc. [n: (Ibid.)] God does not pass over sins unavenged, but so [0112D] begins judgment in our correction that it rests in the damnation of the reprobate. For judgment begins at the house of God. What then is the end of those who have not believed, who feel no temporal scourges? Because they shall perish for ever; whose wickedness was figured in the sin of Cham. To whom it is said: « Cursed be Chanaan the boy, » etc. The father sinning, the son received the sentence, because the wickedness of the reprobate here prospers long, but perishes hereafter.

VERS. 26.-- « Blessed be the Lord God of Sem. » Though he be the God of all nations, yet even among those nations he is called, as by a proper name, the God of Israel, which is from the blessing of Japheth, because in the people of the Gentiles the Church has taken possession of the world, which was foretold [0113A] when it was said: « God enlarge Japheth. »

« Chanaan. » [n: (STRAB.)] From whom the Chananites, who by this curse are prophesied to be driven out of the place of their habitation.

VERS. 27-29.-- « God enlarge Japheth, » etc. [n: (HIERON.)] From Sem the Hebrews, from Japheth the Gentiles. Because therefore the multitude of believers is broad, from breadth, which is called Japheth, breadth is named. But that which he says: « And let him dwell in the tabernacles of Sem, » is prophesied of us, who are occupied in the learning and knowledge of the Scriptures, Israel being cast out.

« God enlarge Japheth, » etc. [n: (ISID.)] Sem, the elder by birth, is himself the Jewish people, from whom the patriarchs and the prophets and the apostles, etc., *as far as* that is, their work.



## CHAPTER X.

[0113B]VERS. 1.-- « These are the generations, » etc. Two sons of Noah being blessed and the middle one cursed, their generations are woven out, from whom seventy-two generations arose: fifteen from Japheth, thirty from Cham, twenty-seven from Sem. To which the Lord sent seventy-two disciples.

« Sem » [n: (ALC.)] Sem, as they reckon, obtained Asia by lot; Cham Africa, Japheth Europe.

« Japheth. » Of the younger son, of whom were born seven sons, who held the northern region from Taurus and Amanus, mountains of Cilicia and Syria, as far as the river Tanais, but in Europe as far as Gadira. Leaving their names to the places and to the nations, of which very many have been changed, while the rest remain.

VERS. 2-6.-- « Gomer. » These are the Galatians, according to [0113C] Josephus, who in Latin are called Gallograeci.

« Thubal. » The Iberians, who are also the Spaniards, from whom Celtiberia, though some suspect them to be Italians.

« Chanaan. » He obtained the land which the Jews afterwards possessed, the Chananites being cast out.

VERS. 8.-- « Regma and Saba, » etc. [n: (HIERON.)] Little by little they lost their ancient names, and what names they now have in place of the old ones is unknown.

« Saba. » Another than the one above, and they differ in the writing: for that one is written with *samech,* this with *schin*; for where we say: The kings of the Arabians and of Saba shall bring gifts, » in the Hebrew there stands [ed: two Hebrew words stand here on Migne's plate; our digitized source has lost both, leaving only the conjunction that stood between them.] *scheba* and *seba.*

VERS. 8.-- « Nemrod. » Who wished to pierce heaven beyond nature. He signifies the devil, who says: [0113D] « I will ascend above the stars of heaven. »[var: Vulg. Is 14:13 reads *In caelum conscendam, super astra Dei exaltabo solium meum*, "I will climb up into heaven, above the stars of God I will exalt my throne."] Nemrod, according to Josephus, seized upon tyranny in a new lust of rule, and was the author of the building of the tower which should touch heaven. « He himself began to be mighty, » or « a giant, » according to others. But Scripture records that giants were born before this. But perhaps the newness of the human race to be repaired after the flood is being recorded, « because he himself began to be a giant upon the earth. »

VERS. 9.-- « A stout hunter. » [n: (ALC.)] That is, an oppressor and destroyer of men, whom he enticed to build a tower against God.

VERS. 10, 12.-- « Babylon. » It is confusion, because there the division of tongues was made.

VERS. 11.-- « Assur. » The empire of the Assyrians, [0114A] which was first under Ninus, who built Nineveh, which on account of its penitence is called *beautiful*.

VERS. 13-20.-- « But Mesraim indeed, » etc. [n: (HIERON.)] Except the Laabin, from whom the Libyans were afterwards named, who before were called Phutaeans; and the Chasluim, who were afterwards called Philistiim, whom we corruptly call *Palestinians*, the other six nations are unknown to us, because they were overthrown in the Ethiopian war. But they possessed the land from Gaza as far as the farthest borders of Egypt.

« Ludim and Anamim. Laabim, Nephetuim, » etc. All these are unknown, either because the nations were destroyed in the Ethiopian war, or because it is not known what names they now have.

VERS. 21.-- « Of Sem. » Of him he speaks last, [0114B] because he pursues his generation, and the line of that generation.

« Heber. » Who nevertheless is the fifth from Sem the son of Noah. Whether because the Hebrews are said to be named from him, or because through him the generation passes over to Abraham? What therefore is the more probable, that the Hebrews are called as it were Heberaeans, or as it were Abrahaeans, is rightly asked.

VERS. 22-24.-- « Assur. » Who built Nineveh.

VERS. 25-30.-- « And there were born sons to Heber, » etc. Heber, *a passer-over*, from whom the Hebrews, that is, *passers-over*. They are true Hebrews who pass over from things present to things to come, from things that fall away to things that abide. These nations, from the river Conus, possessed all the region which is called Geria.



## CHAPTER XI.

[0114C]VERS. 1.-- « And the earth was of one lip, » etc. [n: (AUG., in Gen.)] It was said above that the sons of Noah, or of his sons, were distributed through the lands according to their tongues. But this, which was earlier, he afterwards records by recapitulation. Yet he makes obscurity in this, that he so weaves it together by that manner of speech as though the narration of these things followed upon the things which were done afterwards.

VERS. 2.-- « And when they set out. » [n: (CHRYSOST., in Gen.)] See how the human race cannot subsist within its own bounds, but, coveting more, always seeks greater things. And this is what destroys the human race, that it will not acknowledge the measure of its own nature, but always desires greater things. Since, « they saw, he says, a plain in the land of [0114D] Sennaar, »[var: Vulg. Gn 11:2 reads *invenerunt campum in terra Senaar*, "they found a plain in the land of Senaar."] migrating, and, the former place being left, they dwelt there.

VERS. 3.-- « Come. » They feared a flood again; therefore, Nemrod being the author of it, they wished to set up for themselves a tower in which they should not fear a flood. A foolish and impious boldness, whence followed vengeance and the division of tongues.

[n: (ISID. in Gen.)] The tower, the pride of the world, or the dogmas of the heretics, etc., *as far as* the unity of confession and of faith.

VERS. 7.-- « Come therefore, » etc. He is understood to have said this to the angels; or according to that: « Let us make man to our image and likeness. » For it is subjoined in the singular number: « and he divided [0115A] them, » etc., as there: « God made man to his own image and likeness. »[var: Vulg.
